CHINA TEAS.

Tea it lei Sir Andrew Clarke. For years, whenever my Customers have asked me for a Mild Tea, I have advised the Pure. old Fashioned China which I keep, and the advice has been appreciated. It is very satisfactory to them that such an eminent authority as Sir Andrew Clarke, the London Physician, corroborates opinion. Here is an extract from en interesting lecture, delivered by Sir Andrew the other day, to the Students of the London Hospital: "Tea is a blessed beverage. Ido nOt know what I should do without it. But there is tea and tea, an one of the teas I have in my mind is the representation of all that is physiologically wicked. Igo about town a good deal holding consulta- tions here and there, and about five o'clock, when I get into a place, the lady of the house will say to me : 'Sir Andrew, you look tired, do let me give you a cup or tea.' I say 'thank you very much.' But the tea has stood for half-an-hour, and she remarks, '1 know you do you not like it strong, Sir Andrew. and she then pours about a tablespoonful of the tea into a cup ; and tills thecup up with water. Now I call it positivecruelty to give tea like that to anybody. and I hope you, gentlemen, I will always set your face against it. Tea, to be useful, should be first of air Black China Tea ; the Indian Tea which is being cultivated has become so powertul in its effects upon the nervous system that a cup of it taken early in the morning, as many do, so disorders the nervous system that those who take it actually get into a state of tea intoxication, and it produces a form of nervous disturbance, which is most painful to witness. If you want to have, either for yourselves or for your patients, tea which will not injure and which will refresk, get Black China Tea, putting in the right measure—the old fashioned teaspoonful for each person and one for the blessed pot. Then pour on briskly boiling water, and within five minutes you must pour it off again, or it will become wicked instead of good?' Such is Sir Andrew Clarke's opinion and advise. A few words of warning, however, are necessary to those who have for- gotten what this old fashioned tea is like. The colour of the tea after it is brewed will be found to be much paler than that now in use, and the taste may not be acceptable at first owing to its apparent insipidness ; but if its use is persisted in, it will very soon be found to be much more suitable for the system, and if, in a week to two after its use, a trial is again made of the kind now in ordinary use, you will wonder however you could have been using it,and you will endorse Sir Andrew's statement. This old fashioned China Tea, as recommended by Sir Andrew Clarke, can be obtained now, as always or the last 35 years, at,

THOMAS MOLYNEUX HOUSEHOLD STORES, 37, 39, 41, MARKET-STREET, EARLESTOWN.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

" INDUSTRY must prosper," as the man said when tolding the baby while his wife chopped wood. WHEN is a lighted candle like a tombstone ? When it is set up for a late husband. "WHY is the letter d like a ring ?" asked a young lady of her lover, who was as dull as the generality of .his sex in such a situation. ".Because," added the damsel, with a modest look, "we can't be WED without it." A GENTLEMAN, on being asked what he had for dinner, replied—" A lean wife roasted, and the ruin of man for sauce." What did his dinner consist of? Of course you give it up, and here's the answer— A spare rib and apple-sauce. A LONDON auctioneer has undertaken to "knock down" Ben Nevis, the highest mountain in all Scotland. A GENTLEMAN, when recently married, said, in had grammar, to the officiating clergyman (a con- firmed wit), "I think I have wiqe."—" Yes (replied the other), undoubtedly you have done wise or cther- wise. "

A BUCK, while being measured for a pair of boots, observed : "Make them cover the calf." "Heavens !" exclaimed the astounded shoemaker, imrveying his customer from head to foot, "I have not leather enough." "Miss, will you take my arm?'—" La ! yes, and you too."—" Can only spare the arm, Miss," hastily replied the bachelor. "Then," said Miss, I can't take it, as my motto is to go the whole hog, or none at all." "I HOPE to live to see the day," said Lord Brougham, "when every peasant in England can understand Bacon." "His lordship," replied Cobbett, "had much better hope to see the day when every peasant will be able to eat bacon." THE Dutchman who refused to take a one-dollar bill because it might be altered from a ten, prefers stage travelling to railroads. The former, he says, rides him eight hours for a dollar, while the latter only rides him one. Dee beeples can't aheat him. A LADY thought it would look interesting to faint away at a party the other evening. One of the party began bathing her head and temples with rum, when the lady exclaimed—" For heaven's sake, put nothing on that will change the colour of my hair." WHAT is called by chemists an Evaporating Dish Is the pigeon pie-dish which you take down with you to Epsom, and which, the moment you place it on the ground, evaporates immediately ; more especially if there are any gypsies, or Ethiopian serenaders hanging about your carriage.—Punch.

%HERE may be seen at the present time, in Alfreton, a placard from a tailor, who, in calling the attention of the public to the fact that he intends commencing a clothes club, assures all whc may favour him by becoming members, of having "good charges and a very moderate article." A YOUNG lady who wore spectacles, exclaimed in a voice of sentimental enthusiasm, to a young ploughman who was walking on the road, " Doe you, air, appreciate the beauty of that landscape? Oh see.those darling sheep and lambs skipping about!' —"Them ain't sheep and lambs, themes hogs, miss." _ _

WE all "look down" on somebody. The man ol bullion looks down on the shopkeeper, the shop- keeper on his clerks ; the clerks turn up their noses at mechanics, while mechanics arp above associating with hod-carriers ; seamstresses won't associate with servant girls; servant girls won't speak to flower-girls, while the flower girls think it "low and wulgar " to speak to the little hussies who keep the crossings clean. Who the latter "look down' on we have not heard, though there is no doubt that they consider themselves "far above somebody. AN OPTICAL ILLUSION.—Some years ago, an optician of this city made a glass eye of the propel size, filled it with distilled water, and put therein a small ell. A gentleman of subtle wit, now a dia. tinguished advocate on the wrong side of the temperance question, saw the bauble, was struck an idea, purchased it, and departed. Shortly afterwards, the public were running down Park Row, somewhere, to see "a horse with a snake in its eye," the greatest wonder of the age. Money plentifully to the camp, and the show went on came bravely until the optician inadvertently mentioned the glass eye, and then the whole affair collapsed. —New York Tribune. THE LAST WORST.—An idea of the extremely low degree to which puns have recently sunk may be inferred from the fact that a friend of ours did not hesitate, in speaking of Louis Napoleon, tt affirm openly, before a. crowd of shuddering auditors, that the French people would soon repent the error which they had committed in setting up such a bad precedent WHAT MAY BE MADE OF A NAME.—A gentle- man called Anketil Gray had occasion for a certifi• gate of his baptism, the church being known; but on searching the register no such name could be found. Some mistake was presumed therefore, not in the entry, but in the recollection of the parties, and other registers were searched without success. At length the first register was again referred to, and then upon a closer investigation, they found him entered as Miss Ann Kettle Grey. —The Doctor. A PUZZLED lIIISHMAN.—MT. O'Flaherty under- took to toll how many were at a party :—" The two Crogans was one; meself was two; Mike Finn Was three, and—and—who the deuce was four? Let me see (counting his fiagers)—the two Crogans was one, Mike Finn was two, meaelf was the—and— bedad I there was four of us; but Saint Patrick couldn't tell the name of tke other. Now it meself that has it. Mike Finn was one, the two Crogans was two, myself was three—and—and— by my sowl, I think there was but three of us either all." "THE BOOTS AT THE SUN."—A commercial gentleman, during his sojourn lately at the Sun Hotel, was a good deal struck by the activity and zealous attention show. by " Boots " to his various requirements, and, in the course of a little familiar chat, took occasion to inquire his name. "My name," said the functionary, "is comprised of seven letters : but take the first two away, and there will remain eight." For the information of those readers who know nothing of the Sam Weller of the Sun, we may state that his name is " Speight." —Ulverstone Advertiser. END OF THE DROUGHT.—Mrs. Swieshelrn thus commences a leading article in her Pitsburgli Advertiser—" Thank heaven for the return of the mud, the soft mud—black, yellow, or red, no matter what • colour, every hue of the comfortable old-fashioned mud is welcome, doubly welcome, after its long absence. It tells that the late flinty earth, like the hard and stony. i heart of a sinner, has been reached by the Divine Influence. It sat last melted into sympathy for the sorrcirs of the pow—has relented and opened her bosom to her shivering children, who for a time were cast off, and whose tears full, unheeded, upon her stony breast. The mud, the welcome mud, is her promise to supply their wants, is the sign of her return to her maternal duties." HABITS OF THE NICE YOUNG MAN.—Attends evening parties, and hands the muffins round.— Smiles if he burns his fingers with the kettle.— Plays the flute.—Sings "Do you love me now as then? "—Parts his hair in the middle.—Takes an umbrella with him to an evening party.—Wears goloshes after dusk.—llss a secret passion for gruel,—Writes acrostics, and contributes to ladies' albums.—Curls his whiskers.—ls the "lion Sec." to the "Ladies' Benevolent Mangle Distribution Society."—Keeps a cat, and a regular account of his daily expenses.—His great pleasure is to attend a meeting at Exeter Hall, and his next greatest pleasure is to have his name mentioned "amongst those whom we observed on the platform," &c., &c.—ilis fondest tie, next to an aged grand- mother, is that of his white neckcloth.—Can burn the Overture to Per Freischutz.—Carries a pin- cushion, and acidulated :hops about with him, and is never unprovided with a scent-bottle, for fear of accidents.—Goes out in the rain to fetch a cab.— Doesn't smoke.—llelps mamma's shawl on with the grace of one of Holmes' shopmen.—Has his hair and handkerchief full of scents, and it is a pity: the same cannot be said of his he td.—lfolds skein of silk wilts exemplary patience, turns over the leaves of music with great digital skill, reads novels in a clear secretary-like voice, laughs affetuoso, lisps moderato, jokes with the old maids allegro, .quotes Ilbetry penveroso, runs ladies' errands zreste.sumo, and makes himself generall) itiefulo.— buch are the Habits of the Nice Young Man.— Punvh. " CAPITAL " CHAT.

Busts of Lord Salisbury and the late Mr. W. H. Smith have been executed for the Carlton Club by the Countess Gleichen' wno inherits the artistic talent of her father, the late Prince Victor of Hohenlobe. The bust of Lord Salisbury will be a replica of Count Gleichen's work which stands in the library of the Constitutional Club.

Mr. Fred Leslie left over .£16,000, a big fortune for a poor player. Only Miss Kate Munroe, of recent years, made so substantial an addition to the Trea- sury as he has done in the shape of probate duty. Lessees and managers accumulate much more largely. Mrs. Nye Chat, for instance, who died last year, left £36,000, and Mr. Hobson. of Leeds, was worth £30,000 at the date of his death..

Mr. Edward Arnold, who has become the proprie- tor of the "English Illustrated Magazine," is a nep- hew of the apostle of "sweetness and light." He is already, known u a publisher of educational and general works. Formerly he was with Mr. John Murray,and he helped to start the ill-fated magazine associated with the name of Byron's old publisher. 'Mr. Arnold, who is quite a young man, is already official publisher to the Indian Office. The" well-known dignitary" in the south-west of England, spoken of as likely to get the bishopric of Norwich, is Canon Basil Wilberforce, of South- ampton. The canon's name is a historic one, and his own qualities have made him for long a power- ful figure in the Church. He is an eloquent preacher, a strong temperance advocate like his friend the Bishop of London, and above all a man of singularly large heart.

Sir William Bower Forwood, who entertained Lord Salisbury on Friday, at Raraleh, Blundell- sands, to his birthday dinner, seems to have done justice to an occasion which many a member of the Tory pax ty will envy him. The dining table was connected by telephone with the theatres in Liver- pool, Manchester Leeds, and Loudon, and also with the House of ammons. You had only to lift a seeming table ornament to your ear, and you could drink the music of Meyerbeer, or follow the debate in the Commons.

Sir Joseph Crosland, the new Tory member for Huddersfield, has been wooing that constituency for more than seven years. He had previously fought for the seat in 1885, in 1886, and again last July. Sir Joseph, who was knighted in 1889, is senior partner in the Huddersfield firm of George Crosland and Sons, woollen manufacturers, which was founded by his father. He is chairman of the Huddersfield Banking Company, a large 3mployer of tabour, and a resident in the neighbourhood from the time of Ms birth, now nearly 67 years ago.

At the Cadogan wedding on Tuesday :—Enter an old countryman—probably a tenant on either the bride or the bridegroom's estate—to the pew occupied by Lord Cranborne. Overcome by the festive scene, the old countryman suddenly ejacu- lates, "Eli ! but it begins in joy and frequently ends in sorrow! "I've daughters of my own, and so I knows." After a pause, I was told as I oughtn't to have put on a black stock, and, " ad- dressing Lord Cranborne, " now I sees as youv'e a white one, mister, I see they was right." The occupants of the pew seemed hardly to appreciate being distinguished in this fashion.

p Mr. Walter James, who as the only son of Lord Northbourne has succeeded to the peerage on the death of his father, is a stoutly-built, medium-sized man, with broad forehead, blue eyes, and thick brown hair. Although he has not attained his fiftieth year, Mr. James has served in the House 9f Commons, and sat for the same constituency of Gateshead ever since the general election of 1874: He spoke seldom in the House, but he was an inde- fatigable Radical worker and organiser, and the re-unions of the members of the Liberal party at his house in London were amongst the pleasantest in- cidents of the season. fie is a graduate of Christ Church. Oxford, and in his young days was a famous oarsman.

Mr. Irving's Becket costume, which he has de- clared to be the "most gorgeous he has ever worn," has come from the School of Arts and Crafts at Bedford-park. It is understood to contain .£2OO worth of stage jewels, and, as the average cost of these is 4s. each, some idea of its lavishness may be arrived at. It reminds one of Ingokisby's account of Prince Esterhazy, "all jewels from his jasey to his diamond boots." From Miss Can's direction at Bedford-park also comes Miss Terry's wonderful muslin frock, a fabulous number of yards being em- ployed in the skirt. The school has now added artistic bookbinding to its numerous "crafts," in:all of which it seems doing good wo.L.

Lady Colin Campbell is supposed to benefit under the Duke of Marlborough's will to the tune of £20,000. The bequest is specific enough, but as the net value of the estate amounts to only £3,889, it is not easy to see where the money will come from. Other bequests have to be met out of that sum, £5,000 for the maintenance of Blenheim Palace roof, gifts to old servants, and the residue to the Duchess, with a suggestion that she should lay out £3,000 in restoring Blagdon Church. The Duke's intentions, like those of so many other unevenly balanced men, considerably exceeded his powers. One clause of the will contained a little homily against family exclusiveness. The Duke would not be buried in the family vault. lie preferred to lie among other people's relatives. This is a matter on which we must admit his right to please himself. lie knew the family.

--- Mr. John Lister. the Labor candidate for llalifax, is not exactly a son of toil. His family has been settled in Yorkshire for over four centuries, and he lives now in a very quaint and interesting old manor house, Shibden Hall, lying a mile or two out of Halifax. He is M.A. and a barrister. More- over he is a town councillor, a county councillor, a member cf the school board, and a Fabian Socialist. At Oxford he came under strong religiousinfiuences and in 1872, after an interview with Cardinal Manning, he 'verted to the Catholic Church, having' previously passed to the extreme limit of Ritual- ism in the practices which he was the means of introducing in his parish church. It was as the secretary of a Catholic Working-men's Association in Halifax that he first found the interest in all social movements which has since led him to take an advanced position as a socialist advocate. Mr. Lister is a man of very slight physique and re- served manner. His instincts are those of a student, and he is an ardent antiquarian.

Royal manages require infinite formalities, some times treaties. One was required between Rou- mania and Great Britian for the recent wedding at Sigrnaringen, and this has just been published. Amid much pompous verbiage one little clause stands out with a considerable suggestiveness of its own. It reads as follows : " The High Contracting Parties take note of the fact that by her marriage with H.R.H. the Prince Ferdinand Victor Alfred Mainrad, who professes the Roman Catholic faith, H.R.H. the Princess Marie Alexandra Victoria, according to the due tenor of the law of England, forfeits for ever all hereditary rights of succession to the Crown and Government of Great Britain and Ireland, and the dominions thereunto be- longing, or any part of the same."

Th; Protestant Constitution these realms thillA involves at least one very actual disability to our young princes and princesses, which amply explains their frequent marriages with the Protestant prin- cesses or princes of Germany.

"The Nonconformist Bishop of North London" is the designation which the Congregationalists frequently apply to the Rev. Alfred Rowland, who delivered the "Ancient Merchants'" Lecture at the Memorial Hall on Tuesday. Nor is the designation inapt, for from the cathedral-like Park Chapel at Crouch-end, which is the centre of his pastorate, he exercises a benevolent despotism over the lesser lights of Congregationalism on the northern heights. There is no other.member of his denomina- tion—none more fearless in the denunciation of the crying evils of the time.. none more thoroughly in sympathy with the aspirations of the democracy. lie takes his citizenship into his pulpit, and be- lieves he serves Heaven best who serves man most. He loses no opportunity of proclaiming the doc- trines of Puritan Protestantism, and when, during a visit to Salt Lake City, he was invited to speak in the Mormon Temple, he promptly accepted the invitation on condition that he might say what he pleased. Then the Mormons backed down. Mr. Rowland is on the right side of forty-five, thick set, rather bald, and very fond of a tricycle.

CHAPTER X. AN UNWELCOME SUITOR.

Upon the morning succeeding the visit of young Orkney, Gwendoline signified to Mr. Quillet, whom she seemed to regard as the representative of Squire Markham in matters concerning herself, that she desired a saddle horse for her daily and exclusive use. The old butler carried this demand to his wife in high perplexity. "If Miss (I weudoline wants a saddle horse," sail the housekeeper, "she must have it. " The butler thus encouraged to gratify Miss Winter's desire, remembered that a tenant upon the estate possessed a horse suitable for the purpose in hand, and set out to make a purchase of it. By this time Gwenclolinee was in possession of the habit which had formerly belonged to Miss Mark- ham, and had found that it could readily be fitted to her more slender figure. That afternoon, attended by a servant who was made to do duty as groom, the young girl had a wild ride over the moors, returning home in a state of great exhilaration. The next day she rode again, in the direction of Penistone. She met at a distanfie of some four or five miles from Lonemoor, a waggonette, in which were seated young Orkney and three commonplace young women, his sisters. Her conduct in all things was quiet, gentle, and thoroughly well-bred. She was gracious, sweet, yet proud after a high and noble fashion. Perhaps she would follow in her mother's steps, to meet her mother's fate! All these doubts and fears assailed the house- keeper singly and in troops, making her life a bur- den to her. Sometimes she thought she had done rightly—more often she believed herself foolish and Quixotic. Mrs Quillet had been a farmer's daughter, had received a good common education, and had been regarded as the social 6qual of the farmer's wives, and had even been on visiting terms with the Ork- neys. Now her social atmosphere became clouded. Her forther visitors ceased to drop in upon her. People looked askance at her, as if they thought her unfaithful to the trust the squire had reposed in her, or as.if they censured her singular indulgence to the waif she had so long protected. And although the housekeeper was not .of a social temperament, she felt all this keenly. As was natural, perhaps, to her, she visited her chagrin and anxiety upon the head of the young girl who was their secret cause. She had few pleasant words for Gwendoline. For the most part she was silent and frowt!ne, but when she did speak it was generally in words of censure. Now our little Gwen was not an angel. She was only a warmhearted, highspirited, impulsive girl, wilful and resentful, proud, and possibly wayward =a sweet human girl, with some faults and a thou- sand virtues a girl to be adored, but not a girl to bear meekly undeserved reproofs, and cold glances, and contemptuous smiles. She endeavoured to elicit an explanation of her variable manner from Mrs. Quillet, but the house- keeper had declined to answer, and put an abrupt end to the interview. " I dare say it is edl my fancy," she said to herself on one of these rides, "this coldness on the part of everyone. Why should anyone look askance at the ward of Squire Markham? It's all nonsense. This lonely sort of life will make my mind unhealthy, unless lamon my guard. Of all things, I despise a lackadaisical young woman with a melancholy face and a secret grief." She gave rein to her horse, and indulged in a wild gallop that delighted the animal equally with her- self, and so put to flight by active exercise—her usual resort—all uupleassnt thoughts.

The summer pasged slowly. No visitors came to Lonemoor in these days, even to see Mrs. Quillet. The housekeeper was morose in the highest degree. Her mind seemed settled upon a solemn disapproval of Miss Winter, and she rarely bestowed other than a grimly sour look upon her charge. The butler was uniformly respectful and attentive to the young lady, but the problem of her future and the anxieties of his wife burdened him heavily. The bailiff came often to the great house, and made speeches expressive of his hearty disgust at the state of affairs there, but his wife and daughters held themselves righteously aloof. The bailiff's son came frequently, but Miss Winter carefully avoided him, his appearance being always the signal for her withdrawal from garden or drawing-room. And still, despite all the indications of a suppresed fire, Gwen- doline had no suspicious that all was not right. But this calm was to be broken up. The hour of the girl's awakening was at hand. One day in September she went to ride as usual. She had left off the habit of having a groom to attend upon her, and went alone. The shooting season had commenced. For several years the bailiff had let the shooting during the season, and already sportsmen, with their bags and guns and dogs, were to be met with daily upon the moors. Gwendoline had carefully avoided them BO far, and expected to continue to escape their notice. . . .

She knew that the shooting box, a picturesque little cottage, was some three or four miles distant from Lonemoor, and that it had been taken by a young gentleman, who was now occupying it with a party of friends. Her rambles were made in an opposite direction to the shooting-lodge, and upon this occasion she confirmed to her new custom. The road to Penistone stretched over the moors, a grey, serpent-like line, winding through the gorse and heather, and she rode leisurely along upon it.

No one was in sight anywhere upon the wide pur- ple moors.. The air was crisp, clear, and bracing. It was one of those days when the mere sense of existence is a luxury—when every sense is steeped in a deep and delicious satisfaction. Gwendoline had no errand in view—she rode for riding's sake, and because the sense of freedom was pleasant. Of late, the presence of her groom had grown irk- some, and she had fancied that he had grown less respectful to her. She rode several miles, and then, attracted by some wild wayside flowers, dismounted and plucked them. A TALE OF LONEMOOR.

BY MRS. HARRIETT LEWIS, Author of "The Surylered Hearts," "The Bailiire Scheme," "The Double Life," &c.

CHAPTER IX. MAKING _ AN ENEMY.

The housekeeper and butler exchanged glances of dismay and misgiving before they replied to the interrogations of the Orkneys, father and son. The reputation of the younger Orkney for evil doing flashed upon their mind in the same moment; but they could not refuse to tell what the bailiff and his son could readily ascertain from one of his servants, and Mrs. Quillet said, reluctantly— "Miss Winter is the And we educated, Mr. Orkney. You saw her often enough in her child- hood.'

"What !" ejaculated the bailiff. "Why, I took her for a young lady. She looks-and acts like one. And she is your Princess !' It can't be possible. Don't you know who the girl really is? Who was her father, who are her connections, nor what her station ?" "We do not." said the butler.

"Don't you know her real name?' "We do not. We gave her the name she bears —we invented it—and we have had her educated in Paris as a lady," said the housekeeper. "Her mother was demented and never told her story," said the bailiff, recalling what he had heard at the time of Gwendoline's birth • "and so, for a mere fancy that the vagrant mother was a lady, you have lavished your hard earnings upon the 3hild I What madness! Well, you have reared the little outcast into a fine lady. Now, what are you going to do with her !" " We do not know ourselves," replied Mrs. Quillet, with a troubled countenance. "We shall keep her here for the present." The bailiff was a man of hard commcn sense and the course of the Quillets in regard to Gwencoline excited his strongest condemnation. His cirriusity as to what they purposed doing equalled his sur- prise at what they had done. Therefore, he said— "As you intend keeping her here for the present, how shall you treat her? What room have you given her?" "We shall treat her as a lady," said Mrs. Quillet. "I have, given her the suite of rooms over the library." " Why, these are guest chambers !" The housekeeper assented. "What will the squire say to that ?" "The master left the house in our keeping," said Mrs. Quillet, "and we must answer only to him for our care of it."

The bailiff was angered. "The squire will never like this proceeding of yours, Mrs. QuiHet," he said. "To fill his best chambers with an occupant like this girl ! You carry your privileges too far. You set up this name- less creature as lady of the household. You serve her as if she were your righteous mistress. Now, if the squire were to come ome, this girl would have to leave. If he does not conic home, what is tobe done with her? You have edxcatecl her as a lady. She is beyond your companionship. No one will visit her. No one will recognise her existence. She is doomed to a miserable future in any case. She cannot marry an ignorant labourer, because she is refined and educated. No one else will marry her. The girl will be unhappy all her days, and you two are to blame for it."

will hope for the best," said Mrs. Quillet, trying to hide her uneasiness. "At any rate, Mr. Orkney, we have done what we thought right, and what is done can't be helped." The bailiff gave further vent to his disapproba- tion, and presently took his leave with his son. "I mean to see more of the girl," thought the younger Orkney, as they walked homewards. " I never saw such a beauty in my life. Fresh from boarding school, she can have no lover. I shall manage to see her to-morrow." The Quillets were silent for some minutes after thadeparture of the visitors. Then the housekeeper said in a troubled voice—

"John, it does look as if we had been vez-y fool- ish. Miss Gwendoline is sure to la:: unappy here, without friends, or social y, or pleasures. My only hope is that the squire will come home and see her and take a liking to her. He might adopt her for his heiress, and no one would ever suspect her secret relationship to him." "But, even if she were his heiress, no one would ever forget her ori in," replied the butler, "Birth is of even more account than money. The child ought to have died in her infancy. As she didn't, and as we have educated her as a lady,' we can only hope for tho best. I hope that we haven't laid a train by which to blow ourselves up," he added. "The squire may be angry at our doing what he refused to do, an I turn us out of his house." _

"If there is no place for her here," said the housekeeper, "Miss Gwendoline can become a governe”, I daresay. There's no use in our fretting. Let us trust to Providence.' The squire's unacknowledged granddaughter, without the faintest suspicion of her real position in the house or in the world, took her place naturally as mistress of Lonomoor.

The rooms assigned to her ere pleasant, sunny, and luxurious. But the girl not, as had been expected, confine herself to the-tn. Upon the morn- ing after her return to Lonemoor she ordered the long-disused drawing rooms to be opened, the hol- land covers removed, the library to oe aired, and a habitable look to be given to the best apartments, and the housekeeper and the butler ot eyed her gentle mandates, albeit with great secret dismay and misgiving. It was quite plain that Gwendoline believed her- self to have a right there —a claim upon Squire Markham— and her benefactors dared not- unde- ceive her. In accordance with her habitual prac- tice Gwendoline dressed for dinner upon the second day of her stay at Louemoor, although she was to dine alone.

Her wardrobe had been selected by the mistress of the Paris pensiornat and conformed to the French ideas of suitability to a very young lady. She had no costly silks and velvets, no jewels nor laces, but her garments were all of the finest description and selected with g eat care and taste. Upon this occasion she wore a long wh,te muslin gown, clear and filmy, falling in soft, unbroken, folt.is to the floor. No ruffles, except at the throat and wrists, were to be seen. A broad pink sash, knotted low at her lef t side, and a pink rose in her bronze- coloured waves of hair, constituted her only orna- ments. But these were enough, and served to set off the splendour of her young beauty as effectually as jewels could have done. The butler waited upon her respectfully. No, idea or patronising t.ds brilliant girl, who owed so much to him, ever entered his mind. Her perfect ',reeding, her grave gentleness, her sweet, unfailing courtesy, all impressed him with a conviction of tile iinmense difference between her and him, and he Was not one to presume. After fainner, which was Served with ceremony, Gwendoline went to the drawing-room. The night was warm, and the windows were open. A few wax lights burned dimly in their silver 'sconces. Cool shadows tilled the corners. The young girl sat down at the grand piano and began to play. She dashed into a brilliant ravura, playing with great spirit and force. And then she glided into a scrap of Italian opera, accompanying herself in a voice whose sweetness, purity and strength were, nothing short of marvellous. As he wondrous tones died out upon the air, and the notes rippled slowly under her fingers to their close, a sound of applause startled her. Springing up in alarm, she found herself con- fronted by Claxton Orkney, who bent low before tier, his bold eyes expressing his admiration of her beauty and her musical performance. Gwendoline bowed haughtily in acknowledgment of his greeting, and waited for him to speak. She knew that he was the bailiff's son, and supposed that he had come to Lonernoor upon business. That he had come to call upon her without the knowledge of his father never. occurred to her. His bold glances and his sinister locks caused her in- stinctively to dislike him, and she waited for him to ‘l,, his errand and clepArt, wondering that he should dare enter the drawing-room and her presence un- no ,thced

Young Orkney, knowing the popular story of her origin, and believing her socially far beneath him, wondered at her audacity in taking possession of the best rooms at Lonemoor, and felt for her in the midst Of his sudden and intense admiration a sort of con- descovion.

Thus the two were at cross purposes from the first. "Do you wish to see me ?' demanded Gwen- doline, in her sweet, kw voice, regarding him steadily. "Allow me to summon Mrs. Quillet."

She iook a step towards the bellpull, but young Orkney intercepted her. "Don't call Mrs. Quillet," he exclaimed, "I don't want to see her. I catne to call upon you. I suppose you don't remember me very well, but I saw you once or twice in your childhood. I shouldn't have known you again. though."

LADIES' LETTER.

DEAREST NELLIE, In bonnets and hats we have even threatened changes, and I have actually seen more than one votary of fashion, who did not mind being stared at, appear in a large coalscuttle bonnet. One of these startling bonnets was of fawn-coloured felt trimmed with brown feathers and lined with pink satin, the space under the brim filled up with putllngs of pink tulle. Another bonnet of this type was of black velvet with loops of velvet and a long ostrich feather ; the lining was of pale bite satin ; the frizzy, golden hair of the wearer filled up the open brim. A shape a little deviated from the coalseuttle would have suited to perfection a Puritan maid. It was composed of petunia. coloured velvet, lined underneath with black velvet, and a band of narrow black velvet went around the crown, whilst strings of the velvet a little wider in width were brought from under- neath the "curtain" and fastenea under the chin. The aigrette of plumes and osprey were all in black.

Talking of millinery reminds me that just now once again has risen a craze for timming with violets; this invariaqy happens during January, year after year. The violets are being mounted exactly like those penny bunches which one can buy in the streets, wirli long stalks and a few green leaves enclosing the blossoms. These are rather difficult to manipulate elegantly, being perhaps somewhat stiff, but the artists are being very successful with thorn. A novel idea which obtains in Paris is to powder the artificial flowers with frost —frost it is of a more or less convincing quality, suggestive more I fancy, of the old style of dew. drop with which we used to delight to bedeck flowers. There are only three artificial flowers at :the present moment whose existence is recognised by the fashionable. These are the violets to which I have just referred, shaded magenta roses which have been in existence all through the winter, and red geraniums—the last being, perhaps, the newest corners into favour.

A charming costume showing the bell-shaped ekirt to perfection was of faced cloth of bright blue. The skirt-was trimmed with a shaped band of shaded heliotrope velvet, outlined with a braid- ing of black and gold cocci. The bodice was entirely made of heliotrope velvet, while round the short zouave which falls in full epaulettes over the shoulders wore shaped bands of the velvet outlined with the braid ; the sleeves are entirely of velvet, and the braid again forms a neat finish round the waist. A smart costume, truly, and one which could be effectually copied in half a dozen different materials and colours. The shaped velvet might be, for economy's sake, replaced by cloth of anothee colour; thus, if the dress were of bright gieen cloth, this might be in black, and the braic still in gold and black.

Powdered hair is coming to the fore again foi evening wear; most becoming it is to all types ol beauty and to most types of modified ugliness. Beds poudres will be all the rage this season—ba% entendu not actual costume-balls, which entail ac much trouble, but simply the fancy coiffure which will be worn by men as well as women. The difficulty of getting the powder out of the hair ii not groat, especially when it has been dressed by at experienced hand and is not elogged up with grease Powder is very cleansing, and when it has beer thoroughly brushed out the hair looks bright and glossy, especially when the new and ingeniom whalebone 'brush is sparingly used. As a rule the powdered coiffures are arranged on high oi moderately low cushions, with a long thick curl falling on each side towards the back sometimes the back hair is put up in a long chignon, tied in the. midalo with a ribbon. For a bright, young lace nothing can be prettier or more coquettish than short hair arranged in small ring•curls, lightly sprinkled with powder.

Al! the now materials this seascn seem to be made of two or more colours, and very charming effects are wrought both in shot and raised designs. The same principle exiate .in tweeds and hornespuns, as wdi Its wo.:)11en, and velvet fabrics, tAle former being in a mixture of neutral tints, with bite of red, line, or yellow deftly interwoven. Another novelty is the ;wilfuj. ous cloth and velvet ; it has a ribbed eurface of two colours, which takes the most beautiful kliades when the light falls upon it. Yours sincerely,

To Brighten a Carpet. The best way to do this is said to be the following : Put a half tumbler of spirits of turpentine in a basin of water, and dip your broom in it and sweep over the carpet once or twice, tsnd it will restore the colour and brighten it up until you would think it new.

jug that the Corporation would have been protecting their own interests in taking people from Sankey into the hospital just at this emergency. He hoped it was possible that something might yet be done, and that the Committee might even yet deal with the few cases in Liverpool-road. Councillor FLEMING said the district in question had been intended to be included in the extension of the borough. This the Sankey people resisted, and now they resisted being punished. Alderman Faaxcovit : It was not the people them- selves. Councillor FLEMING said he did not know why the Corporation should assist them,when had they come and helped the Corporation in Parliament this would not have occurred. It was a very regrettable incident. The MAYOR said he believed they might take for granted the decrease of deaths in the smallpox hospital, had been due to reducing the number of persons, and thus giving more air space. He thought they as a Cor- poration should support the Health Committee. Alderman surrox said he was of the same opinion as Alderman Francomb tint it would have been wise to admit patients from Sankey, but the Committee did not consider they should deal with one part of the rural district and mit with others, and therefore in the face of the advice given by Dr. Thomson, it was thought ad- visable in the interest of the whole population not to admit the eases. Vaccination had been the great saving clause with regard to smallpox and no :deaths had oc- curred where vaccination had been successfully per- formed. The minutes were adopted.

SANITARY WORKS COMMITTER. This Committee's minutes were published last week. Councillor REIMAN proposed the adoption. He said it would be remembered by the Council that they adopted at last meeting of the Corporation a proposal to purchase a new boiler for use at Longford works. The Committee bad thought it better to place the destructer boiler on the north or Winwick side of the new works instead of amongst a lot of congested buildings, where it had at first been proposed to put it as the men at the furnaces would there have been confined within four square walls and deprived of air. The Committee re- commended that the tender • of Messrs. Homan and Rogers to erect roof columns and staging complete at Longford, at £558, be accepted. The minutes were adopted.

PAVING AND SEWERAGE COMMITTEE. This Committee recommended that the number of streets be made in accordance with the Private Street Works Act, 1892, and made other recommendatiens which have been already reported including one that a vote of condolence be passed to the widow and family of the late Mr. F. W. Walker. Alderman WRIGHT moved the adoption of the minutes. He said by the death of Mr. Walker they had lost a very valuable and useful official, and the members of the Corporation would all agree with him that the vote of condolence to his widow and children was much called for. He was very sorry that the Corporation was not permitted to do more for the family out of the public funds. He was glad to say, however, that somethihg more was going to be done by the members of that Cor- poration and other gentlemen, and if anyone felt dis- posed to assist he was sure that the Town Clerk or the Mayor would be happy to receive contributions. The MAYOR said Mr. Walker was an industrious,pains- taking man, and he had no doubt the Corporation would have increased his salary this year. Be thought, there- fore, they had acted wisely in continuing the salary up to the 25th of March. He should have liked to see more done by the Corporation,but they must act prudently in dealing with the public money. He must end..rse the expressions of Alderman Wright concerning the deceased. Councillor HUTCHINSON seconded the motion for the adoption of the minutes, and they were confirmed. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE. GAS COMMITTER. Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOUGE moved the adoption of this Committee's minutes. In doing so he said the Town Clerk had received a letter from the Local Government Board (on the 6th February), with reference to the pro- posed loan of £20,000 for the Gas Works purposes. For the purpose of furthering the adjustment of the accounts which had to be made out, he begged to submit the fol- lowing informal resolution " That in accordance with the above letter the Town Council do hereby undertake to repay the sum of £lB,OOO, being part of the said loan of £20,000, within the period of 30 years from the date of the borrowing of such loan ; and also to renay the sum of £2,ooo,making together,with the sum of £lB,OOO the proposed loan of £20,000 within a period of 10 years from the date of borrowing thereof." Councillor BOLTON objected to the passing of the resolution because it had not been included in the printed minutes. He contended that it was not the proper way of transacting such business. Every mem- ber should have had a printed copy of the resolution before him, that he might know what the contents of it were. . . _

The Tows; CLERK explained that it could not have been included in the printed minutee because the letter did not come to hand. If the resolution was passed then they could at once come into the use of the loan, and the officials would be able to include the sum in the accounts which it would be necessary to make out during the month. It was a proper thing to pass the resolution if the members agreed to it. If they. did not pass the resolution that night they would have to wait for a month ; and the adjustment of the accounts would then be delayed. Councillor Bourox : It doesn't matter. I object to this way of dealing with the ratepayers' money. Several members expressed surprise at Councillor Bolton's objection, as it was stated that the resolution merely embodied what the Corporation had already agreed to. Councillor flesannn seconded the adoption of the minutes. Before they were submitted, however, Councillor H. ROBERTS drew attention to the fact that the following tender contained in the minutes had not been advertised in the local press :—" That the tender of Messrs. Bryan, Donkin' and Co.,Limited, for the supply of an engine and patent exhauster at Longford Works at £320 be accepted." He wanted to know if Councillor Fairclough could give any reason for this. A stand- ing resolution which had been passed by the Council demanded that every Committee in asking tenders for anything over £5O should advertise such tender in the local press. The Gas Committee had not done so. It was a common thing for that Committee not to comply with that resolution, and it was the only Committee which did so with such unheeding repetition:

Councillor FAIRCLouca said that it was of no use to advertise in the local papers fpr such tenders as had been quoted. They advertised in the trade papers, and that was sufficient. Councillor ROBERTS : Yes, but then you do not ad- vertise for tenders for the supply of coal in the local Press ; and everyone is quite aware that we have in Warrington many coal merchants who do supply the coal. It is important that in such cases at least the tenders should be advertised. But, setting aside the nature of the tender, it is an obligatory duty on every committee of this Council to advertise for any tender ever £5O, with the local Press. Councillor FAIRCLOUGH did not reply to this, and the minutes were carried. MR. PARR'S OIFT.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY. FEBRUAR Y 11, 1893. .

THE IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. GARDNER, replying to questions, gave an account of the Importation of Pleuro-pnemnouia from Canada in the autumn, and said he would care- fully consider any facts which might be laid before him in support of the desire existing In some quarters for the restoration of the privilege of free entry into this country of cattle from the Dominion. Mr. FENW/CE put a question to the Tice President of the Council about the Operation of the Education Acts and the half-time regulations, and Mr. ULAN], stated that a committee was about to inquire into the subject of school attendance and child labour, and that in the meantime he was willing to introduce a bill to enact that no child under the age of eleven should be totally exempt from school attendance, whatever standard it might have passed. Amongst a number of Government measures intro- duced were bills to amend the hours of labour of rail- way, servants, and to amend the Building Societies Acts. The _ .

Debate on the Address being resumed, Mr. Labouchere's amendment respecting the Uganda Mission came under further consideration. Sir E. GREY said matters of detail could better be dis- cussed in Committee 'of Supply, and Mr. Labouchere, with leave, withdrew the amendment. Mr. WHARTON moved an amendment expressing regret that no mea- sures had been announced for the relief of those affected by the agricultural depression. Mr. JEFFREYS seconded the amendment. Mr. CitimArg said the depression of agriculture was due to to the appreciation of gold. If the principle of bimetallism were adopted he could see so reason why English agriculture should not look for- ward to days of prosperity. Mr. GARDNER spoke in defence of the Government's proposal to hold an inquiry. The debate was again adjourned.

In the House of Lords the Earl of ROSWELL'S, in an- swer to Lord Brassey, stated the instructions which had been given to Sir Gerald Portal for the conduct of his mission to Uganda. That officer was, as far as possible, to maintain peace and order, to develop trade, to help to suppress the slave traffic, and generally. witlaout in- terfering with independent Governments and native customs, to pave the way for civilisation.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Cora-nro—n-s-on Tuesday evening a num- ber of questions were put on the Subject of Railway Rates and Mr. MUNDELL& read a letter which he had received from Sir Henry Oakley., chairman of the Associated Railway Companies, giving an account of the steps which are being taken by the companies te modify their regulations, and said he hoped that Parliamentary interference would be unnecessary. Mr. ASQUITH Informed Mr. W. Kenny that there was not, and never had been, any intention to interfere with the normal operation of the senteace in The Case of Daly, sentenced at Warwick in 1884 to penal servitude for life. In the case of life sentences the ordinary period for reconsideration was at the expiration of 20 years. Mr. T. Healy called attention to the Obstructive Tactics of the Opposition, and Mr. GLADSTONE said he intended to use every effort in his power to limit the general debate on the Address. Debate was resumed on Mr. Wharton's amendment to the Address regretting that no measures had been announced for the Relief of Agricultural Depression.

After seme remarks from Mr.T.W.Russell and Mr. Dillon, Mr. J. MORLIY Said that while there was considerable room for improvement in the general agricultural condi- tion of Ireland, that condition could not be regarded as a critical one, and he could ,not at present pr pose or support a measure for the revision of judicial rents. Mr. Long complained of the barrenness of the Government's programme so far as agriculturists were concerned. Sir William Harcourt advised agriculturists in distress to occupy themselves more with agriculture and less with bimetallism. On a division Mr. Whartnn's amend- ment was defeated by 272 to 232. Mr. Kluft .FlAanis moved an amendment expressing regret that the Queen's Speech made no promise of prompt and effective legis- lation in the interests of

The Unemployed. He claimed that a subject affecting the very life of four millions of people in this country was of as much impor- tance as Irish Home Rule, and said that unless the Government desired to see the experience of Hudders- field repeated in various constituences they would give heed to the question. Colonel Vincsin seconded the amendment. Sir J. GORST said he did not intend to be a party to turning out the Government before the pro- duction of the Home Rule Bill; but he urged upon the Government the necessity of dealing with this question after Home Rule. Mr. Mundella pointed to various bills which the Government had introduced as showing that they were not indifferent to labour questions, and said they should treat the amendment as a question of con- fidence. On a division there voted :—For the amend- ment, 109 ; against, 278 ; majority for the Government, 187. The debate on the address was again adjourned.

In the House of Lords Lord Newton (Colonel Legh) took the oath and his seat on his elevation to the peer- age. Lord Stanley of Alderley asked if the Pest Office authorities had not power to prevent the transmission through the post of foreign lottery advertisements. The Lord Chancellor said it was no doubt illegal to publish advertisements relating to guess lotteries in t big country. It would be competent by warrant to direct that these foreign lottery advertisements in closed wrappers should ho opened and not forwarded to the addresses ; but the trouble and expense would be very great and not likely to prove effectual. He doubted if a warrant could be issued justifying the opening of sealed letters simply on suspicion ; but he would consider the matter. Their Lordships adjourned at twenty minutes to five o'clock.

In the course of the debate Mr. FOWLER said the Government were agreed that the con- dition of the agricultural labourers was such as to call for immediate attention, and they were ready to adopt measures for their relief if the Opposition would let them. The Liberal party had been returned to power pledged to submit a Home Rule measure to the House of Commons. The Government intended the parish or district councils to deal with every one of the questions put before the House in this discussion. Moreover a bill had been promised dealing with the drink traffic, and he was convinced that there was no measure that would do so much to promote the real social well-being of the agriculturist and his wife and children as one that would do something to put a stop to the calamity and curse of drunkenness, which after all was at the bottom of so much of the wretched pecuniary position of the labourers. EQUIPPING THE NAVY., A OLIN WHICH STRIKES AT FOUR MILES.

A Woolwich correspondent states that the War Depart- ment and the Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty have just adopted the new wire quick-firing 6-in.eun for the naval service. The new weapon has been subjected to an exhaustive trial at the Government butts. It is a long gun, and its weight is seven tons. It carries an elongated shot of 1001 b. a distance of 7,000 yards, and will strike an enemy's ship or fortification at four miles distance. Forty cf the new guns have been already manufactured ha the gun factories at Woolwich, and are being issued on various ships in the navy.

ODD-FELLOWSHIP AT WARRINGTON.

INTERESTING PRESENTATION.

An interesting occurrence took place at the fort- nightly meeting of the Admiral Nelson Lodge, of the loctl branch of the Oddfellows' Society on Mon- day night. An emblem of the Order and a certifi- cate for having passed the chairs of his lodge was presented to Bro. Wiattle, P. 0., F.G. Both the em- blem and certificate were beautifully illuminated and framed in massive English gilt frames. Bro. J. Emson, P.P.G.M. seetary, spoke of the admirable manner in which Parast Grand Whittle had conducted the business of the lodge, and the great assistance he had rendered him (Bro. Emson) as secretary and W.G. of the lodge. Bro. Emson then read the inscription on the em- blem of the Order, which is as follows :—" Presented to P.O. Frank G. Whittle by the officers and brethren of the Loyal Admiral Nelson Lodge, No. 3,462, for past ser- vices rendered to the lodge," as well as the hascription written on the certificate—" Presented to P.G. Frank G. Whittle by the officers and members of the Admiral Nelson Lodge, No. 3,482, for his services in the chair, as W.G. during his term of office, July-December, 1892." He then spoke in eulogistic terms of the many good qualities possessed by Bro. Whittle, and said be hoped he would continue to do his duty to his fellowmen. On be- half of the members of the lodge, he had much pleasure In handing to Bro. Whittle the two emblems, which, as they all knew, he so well deserved. (Applause.) Bro. Rigby, D.G.M. said he knew as deputy Grand Master of the district that Bro. Whittle was not only highly es- teemed by the membqrs of his own lodge, but by the whole of the district, and he hoped he would soon occupy the chairs of the district. (Applause.) P.P.G.M. Massey, P.G. Dagnall, and P.G. Boyle also briefly addressed the meeting.—Bro. Whittle said that in look- ing at the pictures he confessed he did not know him- self. He was not aware he had done anything beyond what other officers of the lodge had done. The brethren had bestowed a great honour upon him. He was very pleased to have been admitted a member of the Order, as he had undoubtedly profited by it. At one time he did not think Friendly Societies could be of so much service to a man, but he had found out that they could. He was certainly proud to be the recipient of two such beautiful pictures, and he thanked the brethren for the support they had given him during his term of office. (Applause.)

TERRIBLE TRAGEDY.

MURDER OF A LIVERPOOL WOMAN.

DESPERATE STBUGGLE FOR LIFE.

A murder of a most cold-blooded character, in which two Liverpool persons were the chief actors, was perpet- rated at Burton Latimer, a village near Kettering, , Northamptonshire, on Wednesday night. Last Monday a young unmarried woman named Louisa Johnson, of 24, Bridport-street, Liverpool, went to Burton to nurse her sister, who was ill. She took with her her young child, in respect of which she had recently obtained an affiliation order of 3s. Bd. per week upon Richard Sabey, sewage labourer, 23, Great Newton-street, Liverpoel, who was, she allegea, the father of the child. Sabey denied the paternity, and, as he admits, went ft= Liverpool to Burton Latimer Station, bent on having his revenge. He reached there on Wednesday morning, and lurked all day about Isbrook-cottage, where the young woman was staying, without seeing her. About half- past five the young woman came outfor the purpose of drawing some water from an adjacent well. Sabey appears to have at once joined her, and the two were noticed to walk a short distance together. Suddenly Sabey seized her by the hair and attacked her with a large knife, and the ploughed state of the turf and the fact that some of the fingers of the woman's hand are nearly cut off show that she fought for her life des- perately. She was, however, at length mastered by her antagonist, who is a fine-built fellow, and he succeedel in cutting her throat in a most deter- mined manner from ear to ear. Notwithstanding this terrible injury the poor woman appears to have run for aboht a hundred yards, when she was met by four iron- stone labourers returning from work. She just managed to gasp out "Throat cut," and to point to the retreating figure of Srbey, when she fell down dead. The men at once started off in pursuit of the fugitive, whom they discovered hiding under a hedge. In his hand he held a blood-dripping knife, which he threatened to plunge in-. to the first one who attempted to touch him. Later on he became more passive, and even offered one of the labourers the knife, which he said he had bought on pur- pose for the job. When taken back to the dead body he said, "Yes, I murdered her, and know I shall have to swing for it." He wanted the driver of a trap passing by to take him and the corpse to the neighbour- ing village of Finedon, where he could give himself up to the police. The driver refused, and Sabey, accom- panied by the labourers then rode up to the village on a 'bus, and on arriving there he was given into custody, and was afterwards brought to Kettering. He admitted in the most candid manner to the police that he had committeB the crime, and seemed to take special delight in his blood-drenched garments and hands. Prisoner is apparently 35 years of age, and states that he is married and has a family, his eldest daughter being fifteen. He has served in the army, and is now in receipt of a pen- sion. It is allwed that the child in respect of which the order was made upon Sabey was the second one John- son had borne to him. The order which appears to have incensed Sabey was made upon him so recently as the so 4th of this month.

CHARGE AGAINST A WELSH RECTOR.'

DEFENDANT AND THE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

EXTRAORDINARY SCENE.

At Eerriew Petty Sessions, on Saturday, before Major Corbett Winder and Captain Johns, the Rev. Evan Jen- kins, rector of Manafon, who now resides in Chester, was brought up on a charge of being drunk and disor- derly at Mansion, on the 13th ult. Mr. Maurice Jones, Welshpool, appeared for the de- fendant, and applied for an adjournment on the ground that he had only been instructed for the defence that morning, and that he desired to arrange for witnesses. The Chief Constable of Montgomeryshire objected, ample time having been given for preparations for the defence, as the summons was served on the 23rd. After the court had been cleared and refilled, the Chairman asked Mr. Jenkins why he had not taken steps for his defence earlier. Mr. Jenkins, who spoke in an extraordinary manner, declared that at the last moment Mr. Cartwright, a Chester solicitor, had been compelled to relinquish the case, and he had therefore called on Mr. Jones, of Welshpool, as he came from Clsester that morning. The Chief Constable (excitedly) : Since the court was cleared something has happened between Mr. Jen- kins and myself which causes me to press most strongly ' against an adjournment. I think it is very much better, in the interests of justice and everything else, that the 'case should be dealt with immediately. Mr. Jenkins threatens me with violence, and suggests that I have ulterior motives in bringing the charge against him. I have no other motive than the interests of justice. Mr. Jenkins: I do not know this man. I believe he is but an ordinary policeman from Carnarvonshire. The Chairman: Look here, you must speak is proper and respectful terms of the Chief Constable. Mr. Jenkins : I certainly have met him this morning, and I really would not like to say that he was not influenced by some consideration. The Chairman said he must tell Mr. Jenkins that his behaviour to the Chief Constable was most reprehensible and he was exceedingly sorry and surprised at his language. They did not think he deserved an adjourr,- ment of the case,but they would adjourn it for a month. Mr. Jenkins: Keep an equal mind, Mr. Corbett Winder. Keep an equal Mind, sir. The Chief Constable : Silence ! If you insult the court we hhall have to take charge of you. Mr. Andrew, a gentleman farmer, one of the wit- nesses, rose and said that his mother, who was an old woman of seventy years of age, had been put to a great deal of anxiety through the defendant, and he asked for her protection during the month intervening before the next hearing. The Clerk said that if she had any reason for being protected, she herself must make the application. When Mr. Andrew was offered ss. costs for her attend- ance, he would not accept so small an amount. Mr. Jenkins : It is ridiculous. Mr. Jenkins then threw down a sovereign to pay the costs with the utmost indignation, winked energetically at the court for several seconds, bade the magistrates and the Chief Constable individually a most pronounced " Good morning," and disappeared.

THE EIGHT HOURS BILL.

The Mines Eight Hours Bill is issued with the Parlia- mentary papers. It has been prepared and brought in by Mr. Seorey, Mr. Pickard, Mr. Woods, Mr. Leake, Mr. Jacoby, Mr. Abraham, Lord Churchill, Mr. Cremer, Sir Charles Dilke, Mr. Phillips, Mr. Randall, Mr. Conybeare, and Sir Albert Rollit. The two essential clauses of the bill areas follows :—" Any employer, or agent of any employer, employing or permitting to be employed any person in contravention of this enactment shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding 40s. for each offence, to be recovered in the same manner in which any penalty under the Acts relating to factories and workshops is re- coverable Any person shall not in any one day of 24 hours be employed underground in any mine for a period exceeding eight hours from the time of his leav- ing the surface of the ground to the time of his ascent thereto, except in case of accident."

PLEASANTNESS.

If home is to be the very dearest spot on earth, it can only be such if the mother or wife brightens it with the sunlight of her cheerful smile. This radiance must be natural, and the genuine fruit of peace, kindness, and serenity. When things go right, in the kitchen, the aundry, and the bath, the good housewife's face is lit up with a loving, smiling calm, This brightness always follows the use of

THE LANCASHIRE -WATCH COMPANY

LIMITED.

The report of the Lancashire Watch Company, Ltd., states :—Your directors are pleased to report a net 'balance of profit, after paying the interim dividend on preference shares to 30th June last, of £4,841 17s. 4d. (including £1,021185, from last year), to be dealt with at the end of the fourth year's working, and the direc- tors recommend the payment of a dividend of 6 per cent, on the ordinary shares of the company, and a further dividend on the preference shares at the rate of six per cent, per annum, and that £5OO be written off machinery account. These payments would absorb the sum of £4,517 14s. 2d., leaving a balance of £324 3s. 2d. to be carried forward. Your directors are pleased to announce that complete watches are now being made in considerable numbers, there being a good demand for the same, and it may be confidently anticipated that a substantial increase of profit should accrue to the com- pany henceforth. During the year under review the new machine shop has been fully equipped with the necessary tools and machinary, and is now in full work- ing order, and the three-story building is now being fitted win the requisite machinery, and from time to time operatives will be introduzed into the various departments. We have pleasure in announcing that the whole of.the branches of watchmaking are now established on the premises with the exception of case- making, which will be proceeded with in due course.

THE ATLANTIC RECORD OF 1893.

The year 1893 is likely to be one of record making and record breaking on the Atlantic, aud the racing of the year will begin with two matches in this month. On Wednesday, February Bth, the City of New York and the Majestic both left Liverpool for New York, and on Wed- nesday, February 22nd, the City of Paris and the Teutonic in like manner leave the same day. The four fastest steamers now on the Atlantic will (says the "Daily Graphic ") thus be pitted against each other for the last time, because the New York and the Paris return not to Liverpool but to Southampton, and it may be a question of some interest also—Will they break the record from New York to Southampton on their first inward voyage ? The fastest up t ) date is that pf the Furst Bismarck, in June, 1892,6 days 11 hours 50 min., an average of 19.50 knots an hour. It is of some inter- est to note that the two steamers which are to start from Liverpool for New York together on February 22nd, the City of Paris and the Teutonic, have made the two fast- est westward runs hitherto, and the best twenty-four hours' run of each steamer is exactly 530 knots.

AN AMERICAN RAILWAY "HORROR."

NEW YORK, WEDNESMY.—A serious accident occurred last night on the Cleveland, Columbus, Cincinnati, and Indianapolis Railroad, near Pana, Illinois. The night was extremely cold, and the frost seems to have broken a rail on a trestle bridge. A train from St. Louis was I thrown off the rails and the wrecked carriages caught fire, and the entire train, which included two sleeping cars, was burned. Thirty-one passengers were injured. They were rescued with the utmost difficuly from the blazing train. The baggeman met with a terrible fate. He was pinioned among the trunks in the van and burned to death before the eyes of the train officials and passengers, who could do nothing to help him owing o the fierceness of the flames. Amongst the injured were a wornan and her four children. They were ac- companying to their home the body of the husband and father,who had died at a distant town. The coffin, with the corpse,was burned to ashes. The intense cold added greatly to the sufferings of the passengers.

THEFTS BY A WARRINGTON RAILWAY OFFICIAL.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAVY PENALTY.

At the Borough Police Court, on Saturday morn- ing, Robert Barlow, of Nicholson-at., was charged on remand with stealing on the 31st January, and on the 13th, a quantity of china and 41 packets of soap,valued at .124,fr0m the premises of the London and North-Western Railway Co. Mr. P. Davies appeared for the prisoner, who pleaded guilty. Mr. Fenna represented the Railway Company. Detective Osborne, who is in the employ of the Rail- way Company at Warrington, stated that about eight o'clock on the morning of the Ist of February, he re- ceived information from a foreman carter named Wil- son, about some soap baying been stolen from the goods department. In consequence of that information, he went to the prisoner's house. He said to the prisoner, " I suppose you know what I have come about?' Prisoner replied, " Yes ; the soap." Witness then searched the house, and SAW the tablets of soap in the cupboard. On the table he also found a jar of marma- lade and another one in the pantry. He proceeded to the cellar and there found the china and a brush. He charged prisoner with stealing the articles, whereupon the latter remarked, "Yes, they do belong to the Com- pany. I brought the brush home to dry it, and the china I brought in a hurry,intending to take is back."— Mr. Fenna : You were aware that the china ought to have been taken to Mrs. Greenall, at the Rectory, Grap- penhall p—Witness : Yes, sir. Mr. Davies said that all he could say in the prisoner's defence was that he had borne an excellent character at Messrs. Garnett and Sons, and at Mr. T. Sutton's, where he had been employed before he started working on the railway stores. He had been under the care of Dr. Adams for the past three months, and was being at- tended for an affection of the brain. The doctor would tell them thet the man was quite a kleptomanisc. His (the prisoner's) wife had for some time been seriously ill on account of his conduct. The prisoner was still under the impression that he had done nothing wrong. If he were sent to prison it was not so much he who would have to suffer, as the poor wife, who had five children to support. Several of the articles which the prisoner was charged with having stolen were really his own property, some of which the wife had bought her- self. Inspector Spinks, called by Mr. Fenna, stated that he charged the prisoner with having stolen the articles, and he replied " I have nothing to say." He (witness) then placed all the stolen articles before the prisoner, and asked him to put aside those which he had taken from the railway company, and those which he had not. The man did so. Dr. Adams said that he had been attending the pri- soner for some time, and found that he was suffering from a temporary disturbance of the mental equilibrium. fle could not say whether that was a sufficient reason for his having stolen the articles. Alfred Wilson, foreman in the goods department of the London and North-Western Railway Company at Warrington, showed an apron which load been worn by the prisoner at work. The apron when on looked "quite innocent," but when taken off and examined was found to be a kind of sack, with an opening at the top into which articles could be slipped with the great- est ease. Mr. Wilson went up to the table on which were the stolen article, and gave the Bench an exhibi- tion of " how the trick was done," but Mr. Davies said he had never seen such an unseemly proceeding in a police court.

The prisoner's son stated that his father's conduct was very strange at times, and before he was attended by , Dr. Adams he used to go to the Infirmary. The Bench, after a snort deliberation, sentenced the [ prisoner to three months' imprisonment on the first charge and to three months, on the second, both with hard labour. Mr. Burgess (chairman) said the magis- trates had just received a letter which plainly showed that the prisoner had been carrying on the same kind of business for a number of years, and it might well have been put *step to long ago.

KEATING'S COUGH LOZENGES.

"94, Commercial Road, Peckham, July 12, 1889. " Dear Sir,—l am a poor hand at expressing my feelings, but I should like to thank you. Your lozenges have done wonders ia relieving my terrible cough. Since I had the operation of ' Tracheotomy' (the same as the late Emperor of Germany, and unlike him, thank God, I am still alive) per- formed at St. Bartholomew's Hospital, no one could possibly have had a more violent cough; it was so bad at times that it quite exhausted me. The mucus, which was very copious and hard, has beers softened, and I have been able to get rid of it without difficulty.—l am, sir, yours trily, J. HILL."

UTTERLY UNRIVALLED.

The above speaks for itself. From strict inquiry it appears that the benefit from using Keating's Cough Lozenges is understated. The operation was a specially severe one, and was performed by the specialist, Dr. H. T. Butlin, of St. Bartholomew's Hospital. Since the operation the only means I relief is the use of these Lozenges. So successful are they that one affords immediate benefit, although from the nature of the case the throat irritation is intense.

The A LONDON newspaper has been Emoluments gathering information and making of Footballers. calculations with regard to the re- muneration of football players, and it comes to the rather remarkable conclusion that consider- ably over a million pounds a year is paid in sala- ries to the athletic young men who " chase the leather' on Saturday afternoons. This, too, of course, only applies to those who play the Asso- ciation game, for the Rugby code does not re- cognise professionalism, and prohibits payment of Players. Allowing for exaggerations—and there 13 undoubtedly room for a very liberal discount on the figures quoted—it is pretty clear that foot- ball is not the worst paid of the professions. In fact, the supply of really expert Associationists falls short of the demand. It is the custom, it seems, for some of the wealthier organisations to ala'aYs have agents on the look-out for likely Young men, and when is found the inducements held out to him are pretty lavish. Certain parts of Scotland are the most Prolific recruiting grounds, and it was a young ketchman from Renfrewshire,at one time a mem- ber of the Woolwich Arsenal Club, who imparted the information to the newspaper interviewer. Scetchmen are virtuous in regard to football—or l'ather they assume a virtue which does not exist Ct for they interdict professionalism and call them- elves "amateurs." But, says the Woolwich ketchrnan, it all "comes to the same thing in the the players do not receive waaesf but there .are always tips on the sly in every club." He leraself when he transferred his talents to the Woolwich Arsenal club received a bonus of seventy Pounds ; three pounds a week during the football I.,.aasen, and two pounds a week in the close season. :11 return for these fairly substantial emoluments, "Played in two matches a week upon an average, 4" " trained " assiduously,the trainin trange to 84Y, including an occasional half-hour's exercise Zith the skipping-rope. On the whole,he considered e money well-earned, for he adds "I have been fil,ettY well kicked and battered and torn to pieces. titiave had sprained ankles and sprained knees by Ige score ; I have had black eyes by the hundred; have been kicked over the eye, back, head, and !Pine innumerable times, and have put out my gligers oftener than I can calculate." After this !lightly imaginative account of the perils of foot- he gave it as his opinion that a man, with av °rage luck, might last as a professional football 131a3'er till he was about forty years of ago, always ,Previded, of course, that he was not killed in the arat season."

Starving A deputation which waited upon C:.1)°01 Mr, ACLAND, the head of the Education Department, on Wed- day, in favour of School Boards being ern- eowered to provide free meals and froe clothing for necessitous children, met with a sympathetic reception. Mr. ACLAND has already infused a and a more liberal spirit into the adminis- qation of the Education Acts, and he is not one of those politicians who will reject a proposal avirePlY because it is new, or even because it in- iv" a somewhat startling departure from old Methods. At the same time he did not, and it wa's Probably not expected that he would, alto- gether fall in with the suggestion of the deputa- tion. The question of providing food for school children is 9.s he said, a very difficult problem That in many poor and populous districts the free. education which is imparted is robbed of much 14 its utility by the insufficient nourishment of the children canrwt be disputed. It is said that in .4cilldon alone there are, according to School Board qatistics, at times not fewer than thirty thousand lChildren sent to school without having had any 4.reakfast. What a disastrous waste of educa- wonal force this must represent, for it is idle to 1-)ect that ch ildren who are pining for food can ttash imilate the mental pabulum that is offered to ena- Something has already been done by pri- te generosity to feed these starving scholars, but Where the need is so great and widespread the ;.`;)rta of private individuals almost of necessity 'lll far short of the requirements. In Paris, it is 4 id, the evil is much more effectually dealt with by a combination of municipal grants and private i"neence, and Mr. ACLAND spoke in favour if gli Municipal authorities being empowered , e similar grants. As he said, without food vo. without healthy surroundings they might as 11 not try to carry on education at all.

A labourer named Henry Stowles, of Victoria-street, was sentenced to pay a fine of Ws. 6J. or 14 days' hard labour, by the Widnes Bench on Monday morning, for stealing a cap belonging to Hannah Murkle, from a stall in Widnes market, on Saturday night. The prisoner pleaded that he was drunk at the time. -
