THE WIDNES STABBING CASE.

Before the Mayor (Alderman Caossage) and Mr. H. Wade Deacon, at Widnes Police Court on Monday, a labourer named Jno. Robinson, of no fixed abode, was charged on remand with stabbing a fellow lodger, named Wm. Edmondson, in a lodging-house in Lugsdale road, on the 28th January. The circumstances have been reported in this journal. The prisoner was com- mitted for trial. CONCERT AT WIDNES.

In aid of the building fund of St. Paul's Parochial Rpoin. a concert was given in the Drill Hall,on Wednes- day evening, under the presidency of the Mayor (Aid. Gossage). There was a good attendance. The pro- gramme was sustained by Mr. Josef Cantor's Company, consisting of Ma:latm4 Laura Smart, Miss Wayman, Messrs. Ben Roberts, F. Owen, V. H. Needham, and Mr. Cantor. On the motion of the Rev. G. H. Garrett, the Mayor was thanked for his patronage and help. JOSEPH PRlTcHenn,Wlatehmaker and Jeweller, 51, High- et., and 4, Bridge-st., Runcorn.—Advt. A WIDNES FLATMAN DROWNED..

On Thursday afternoon, Mr. Sampson, city coroner, Liverpool, held an inquest on the body of Thomas Hayes, 58 years of age, a flatman, who lived in John-st.,Widnes. On Tuesday night a flatman named Owen O'Neil said he was in his flat in the Nelson Dock. He heard a splash,and on rushing out saw the head of the deceased, whose fiat was next to witness's appear on the surface of the water. Deceased was got out with a rope, but he shortly after- wards became unconscious and died. A watchman em- ployed on the dock said h observed him go to a ladder leaning on a flat. He did not know anything about the deceased till he helped to pull him out of the water It appeared from the medical evidence that death was caused by shock due to immersion.—Tho jury returned an open verdict.

RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY. The half yearly meeting of the members of this society was held on Tuesday evening, in the Lacy-street School, Widnes. Mr. Rogerson presided. The balance sheet for the past half-year was submitted and adopted. It showed that the total sales amounted to £43,583 ss. 2d., an increase of £2,567 15s. 9d. on last half year, and an increase of £4,39 2s. 6p. on the corresponding half year. The amount disposable after charging all ex- penses, including depreciation and interest on capital, deducting the checks purchased, left the net amount £6,183 is. 10d. They recommended a dividend of 2s. 10d. in the £ on £43,5040f check account books brought in, which would amount to £6,175 16s. 4d. leaving a balance of £7 ss. 611 to the reserve fund. A proposal with regard to giving a donation to the Runcorn Tech- nical Institute was discussed, but ultimately it was resolved to postpone their decision until the annual meeting in August. SMALLPDX AND SCARLET FEVER.

The medic-al officers of health- (Drs. M‘Nicoll and Rodertson) reported to the St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday afternoon, that during the past fortnight there had been 129 births, equal to a rate of 44 per 1,000 per annum of the population, and the deaths numbered 67, equal to a rate of 23.28, against 25.27 in the previous fortnight, and 19.50 in the corresponding period of last year. There had been one death from smallpox and 11 notifications of the same disease. There had also been 26 notifications of scarlet fever. The death from small- pox occurred in an unvaccinated girl who contracted the disease from Runcorn,and of the eleven notificatior.s four were probably directly imported from Runcorn, and seven occurred as secondary cases from Manor-st., where several cases had not originally been notified Should no fresh importation occur there would probably not be many more cases. At the hospital there were 14 smallpox cases under treatment. FUNERAL OF MR. GEORGE MOULTON, RUNCORN. On Monday afternoon,the body of Mr. George Moulton, The Heath, Runcorn, was interred at the Runcorn ceme- tery. Whilst following his employment on Runcorn Hill, deceased caught a chill about a fortnight ago, which rapidly developed into bronchitis to which he succumbed on Thursday the 2nd inst. He was 61 years of age, and having resided in the neighbourhood for more than half a century, he was well known by a large circle of friends by whom he was held in the highest respect. For many years he was engaged in the quar- ries, but more recently he had acted as supervisor of Runcorn Hill, under Mr. Charles Linaker, agent to the Marquis of Cholmondeley. He was a devoted Wesleyan but his health had prevented him from regularly attend- ing chapel of late. He was of a reserved disposition and quiet manner. The funeral was attended by the fol- lowing gentlemen :—Messrs. J. F. Moulton, Wm. Moulton, Arthur Moulton (sons), Joseph Cross (son-in- law), Wm. Ford,John Holford (Flint), Geo. Ainsworth (brothers-in-law), Geo. Dodd, John Bosworth, Frank Rowe, Thos. Ford, Thos. Widders, Wm. Stanway, Thos. Crosby., B. Crosby, T. Bowyer, D. H. Jones, Thos. Molyneux, M. Sweetman, Jas. Gregory,, Fred Wilson, John Sefton, All. Hunt, fly. Willis, S. Smith, Bagsliaw, Worrall, Garner, Dr. Robinson, and the Rev. W. -Arrow- smith, who performed the burial service. The bearers were Messrs. John Ford, Thos. Ford, Jos. Ford, Wm. Ford, Win. Dunhavin, Jas. Dunbavin, J. Cottrall, and Alfred Dutton (nephews of deceased). PRESENTATION TO MR. MEADOWCROFT, OF RUNCORN.

A deputation consisting of Messrs. T. Manning, Geo. Tomlinson, P. Hopwood, and C. Rowe, representing the workmen enaployad at Runcorn Docks, waited upon Mr. Meadowcroft, local agent to the Manchester Ship Canal Co, (Bridgewater Undertakings) on Saturday night and presented bim with a handsome solid silver cake basket on which was inscribed:—" Presented to Mr. and Mrs. Meadowcroft on the occasion of their silver wedding, by the foremen and workmen of the BridgewaterSanals Undertaking, Manchester Ship Canal Co., Mneorn Docks."—ln acknowledging the gift, Mr. Afeadowcroft referred to the good feeling that had existed between himself and the men during the fifteen years he had occupied the responsible position of agent. They had now almost arrived at a very important period in filo listory of Runcorn as a port. When the Ship Canal was opened throughout for traffic, he thought there would be many changes; some portion of the traffic which was now dealt with would probably go direct to Manchester ; on the other hand, they would have a new class of traffic to replace it, and it would be of such a character, and he trusted of such dimensions, as to far exceed the anticipations of anyone who knew the capabilities of the place at the present day. With a continuance of the same rigorous management at headquarters, he believed the Bridgewater Canals Undertaking at Runcorn would become a most valuable and important item in the pre- perty of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. It had splendid water and rail communications to all parts of England, and geographically it was well situated for a large export, as well as a large import trade. The men, therefore, might have good hope for the future, and he trusted they would benefit by whatever changes took place. He again cordially thanked them on behalf of Mrs. Meadowcroft for the presentation they had been pleased to make, and said he should treasure it as an agreeable link in his association with the port and the workmea. The proceedings that terminated.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 11. 1893.

WESTON BIBLE AND MUTUAL IMPUOVEMENT CLASS

On Sunday night last. in the Wesleyan schoolroom the Rev. J. Pulfora Williams of Runcorn, gave a lecture on " Lessons from Mythology or the religion of the ancients." Mr. James Ford occupied the chair.

THE HUNCOUN STABBING} CASE.

The boy Th-o;;;Eisiier, aged 13 was on Wednesday again remanded, on the charge of stabbing Arthur Royle aged 10 years. Superintendent Atherton said the injured boy was improving slowly, and would not be able to appear for some weeks yet. 'IRE SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. The members of the senior classes, the teachers and choir held a tea party on Wednesday evening, and after- wards a meeting took place under the presidency of Mr. Shaw, the missi,mary. MOLESTING A GIRL ON RUNCORN HILL. EXTRAORDINARY DEFENCE. Yesterday at Runcorn Police Court, before Mr. Henry Reynolds,a young man named Henry Green- way, aged 20, labourer on the Ship Canal, living at Brick Kiln Houses, was charged with indecently assaulting Mary Pulford, aged 16 years, domestic servant to Mr. J. E. Meredith, Stanley Villas.—The girl stated that her parents lived at Weston, and on Thurs- day night about seven o'clock she W 39 en her way to see them, when near the Battery on Runcorn Heath, she saw the prisoner leaning against a gate. She bade him " Good night" and he respondad. She then said " Are you Dodd from Mrs. Moran's at Weston," and tie replied "No." She started to walk sharply, and he walked up to her side, and then she crossed over the road to avoid him, when he went behind her and assaulted her. She fell on her knees and screamed, and the prisoner ran away. A man named George Dodd and P.C. Benfield had heard the screams and the latter seeing the pri- soner running away apprehended him. In answer to the charge he said" I don't know what made me do it. I never did such a thing before." Yesterday he made the following statement to the magistrate : "I got properly wet through yesterday and it new to my head. I started to go to Weston but altered my mind. When I stopped against a wall, my head was that had that I did not know where I was for about half-an-hour. When this girl came up I was thinking of committing suicide, my head was so had. It came on all at once. I followed the girl along the fields and she spoke to me and then all of a moment with my head being so bad, I flew up against her, and she then started screaming and Iran back." The prisoner was committed for trial to the Quartet Sessions.

RUNCORN WATER BILL._

From an advertisement in another column it will be seen that a meeting of ratepayers is called for Thursday evening next, for the purpose of confirming the action which the Board of Commissioners have taken in regard to the proposed purchase of the undertaking of the Runcorn, Weston, and Halton Waterworks Company. The question at issue is a momentous one for the pros- perty and well-being of the community, and no doubt the Commissioners will be prepared to lay facts and figures before the ratepayers, upon which they can base their judgment. It is an axiom which whenever acted upon has proved beneficial, that the water supply should he under the control of the local authority and not in the hands of a private company.

A DROWNING MYSTERY AT RUNCORN.

On Saturday, Mr. Worslev, coroner, held an inquest at the Howe Farm, Norton, on the body of Annie Leaver, of 68, Queen-st., Runcorn, who was found drowned in the Bridgewater Canal, at Norton, on Thursday night.— Win. Lever,the husband, said the deceased left home on Tuesday night, and he had not afterwaads seen her alive. Her health had not been very good for some time past, but he knew of nothing that would have caused her to commit suicide. Her children had left home t 3 procure work in St. Helens, and this might have preyed on her mind as she was very fond of them. They had lived together very happily.—George Rigby. game- keeper, deposed to having seen the deceased walking along the towing path of the canal, and Wm. Percival described how he found the body in the canal near Nor- ton Priory.—The body had been mangled by a steamer. —An open verdict of "Found drowned " was returned. DEATII AND FUNERAL OF MR. R. LEA, FORMERLY OF RUN- CORN.

On Thursday afternoon the mortal remains of Mr. Richard Lea, of Hope Villa, Little Sutton, near Chester, were interred in the family grave in the burial ground of Tarvin Church. The deceased gentleman who had attained the age of 71 years died on Monday evening after a long and severe illness. Born at Kelsall, on October 11. th, 1821, he came to Runcorn in 1839, and in the establishment of Mr. W. B. Gibson, ironmonger, carrying on business at the premises now occupied by Messrs. Handley and Beck he served an am:), enticeship of seven years. In February 1852 Mr. Lea commenced business -on his own account at the corner of Church-st. where he carried on a successful trade for over 30 years when he left the management entirely to his son Mr. W. E. Lea, who as an electrican and engineer, has comdder- ably extended the operations of the firm. From 1866, when he succeeded Mr. Stelfox, until 1889, Mr. Lea held the position ot postmaster in which he won the respect and esteem, of the public by his unvarying genial courtesy. Outside his business and official duties the deceased gentleman took considerable interest in public affairs. For three years, 1877 to lan, he was a member of the Board of Commissioners, ;mad he also served the ratepayers for some years as a guardian of the poor. On the formation of the School Board he was elected a member, and in this capacity he acted for a peridd of nine years. A firm believer in the volun- teer movement, Mr. Lea joined the Runcorn corp at the commencement of its career, and maintained his connection for twenty years, having attained the rank of Lieutenant some years before he retired. On his retirement he was presented with a handsome testimonial. In politics a staunch Conserva- tive. Mr. Lea was in religion a churchman, and held the position as churchwarden of Holy Trinity Church for 16 years, and he was also a valued manager of the day schools, for which institution he also acted as treasurer. It is hardly necessary to state that Mr. Lea was one of the best known gentlemen in town, and he was widely respected and esteemed alike for his good qualities of head and heart. He leaves a widow, two sons.and a daughter to mourn his loss. The funeral service on Thursday was conducted by the Rev. Dr. Preston, Vicar of Holy Trinity, Runcorn, and amongst those present were :—Mr. William E. Lea, Mr. Herbert Lea (son), the Rev. J. Jervis (son-in-law), Mr. Lea (of Hatton, (brother-in-law, and formerly of Holy Trinity), Mr. Christie, of the Post Office staff, Runcorn, and Dr. Robinson, medical attendant. The arrang-ments were ably executed by Mr. W. H. Rogerson, draper, Bridge- street, Runcorn. On the coffin Were a number of beau- tiful wreaths and crosses. These were from Mt. and Mrs. Lea, Runcorn ; Mr. and Miss Lea, Halton ; the Rev. and Mrs. Jervis ; Mr. and Mrs. Morrison, Water- loo ; Mr. and Mrs. Whittaker, Runcorn ; and an exqui- site WON nom the Postmaster and staff at Runcorn.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN IT ItUNCOItN

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

Smallpox is still with us in Runcorn, but so secretive are the health authority and theirofficials that there is no means of giving accurate informa- tion as to the extent ot the epidemic. I believe it is a fact that there are upwards of twenty patients in the hospital at the present time, and a medical practitioner ventured the statement the other clay that there were altogether about fifty cases in the town. The disease is also very prevalent in the surrounding district, and the Rural Sanitary Authority are dealing with the outbreak in a man- ner calculated to inspire confidence that they will succeed in stamping it out. It behoves the townspeople to take every precaution in regard to cleanliness and diet, and not to spare disinfectants about their houses. Discretion in moving about the town and mixing with company would also appear desirable, judging from the vagarie% of the smallpox patient at Stockton Heath, who,it is to be hoped, will be made to pay dearly for Cie wilful exposure of himself of which he is said to have been guilty.

It was announced by Mr. James Dobson about two years ago, that tne Runcorn hospital was prac- tically free, and there was a considerable " flourish of trumpets" over the announcement. At the meet- ing of the Guardians. on Tuesday, Mr. BraudrAli stated that the only patients who were paid for in the institution were the paupers, and there have been statements Made in the town that lend colour to such an assertion. It has been alleged that the removal to the hospital of several sufferers from smallpox has been delayed until they had obtained orders which made them pauper inmate,. Further enquiry would probably reveal that other patients are charged, but afterwards the charges are remit- ted. As a member of the Health Committee, Mr. Lea asserted that that body made isolation of the patients their first duty ; cost was a secondary consideration. It was justly argued that as the people are removed to the hospital for the public good, no charge whatever should be made ; and the Local Government Board Inspector, who was pre- sent, said many authorities took that view of their responsibilities.

The reply of the Education Department to the inquiry of the Runcorn Guardians, as to the pay- ment of fees for pauper children in attendance at fee-charging Nonconformist schools, was what might naturally have beeki expected. A demand for free education in any locality, made in proper form, will cause an order to be forwarded from the Department to the School Board to provide the requisite accommodation, if the Denominational schools will not supply the want. If the denomin- ationalists are sincere in their desire to educate, they would make the sacrifice of the pence to keep the children under their wing., In Runcorn, how- ever, an extension of the Board'Achool is demanded by the excess of those in attendance over the certi- fied accommodation, and this is a problem the new School Board will have to deal with at an early date.

The first meeting of the new School Board occu- pied but a few minutes altogether. When Mr. Iwison and Mr.• Taylor made an appearance the appointment of chairman and vice-chairman had been made. Mr. James Pritchard was re-appointed to his former position, and Dr. McDougall took the place of the Vicar in the vice-chair. The Vicar was absent from town attending convocation, and Dr. Gastaldi, who has only recently recovered from a severe illness, had had a long and wearying day at Dutton Workhouse, and he was not in a fit condi- tion to attend another meeting in the evening.

There are many who believe that a reform in our present funeral customs is needed, and desire to see It carried out, but give no practical help to the movement. Perhaps the remarks made by Dr. Nor- man Carr at the last monthly meeting of the Fune- ral Reform Association, will incline them to take a more active part in the movement. He said that prevalent burial customs were directly contrary to the Christian faith, dispiriting, injurious to the health of the bereaved, cruelly costly, and noxious to residents in the vicinity of the buried dead. Christian ministers taught that death was no break in the continuity of life, but a transference into a higher sphere of being; yet, after a death, every I room was darkened and the clothing worn was of the despest black. Nothing could be more depres- sing, dispiriting, and harmful. With the light of heaven shut out, the digestion was impaired, and the whole system lowered. The physical was added to mental distress. The body re-acting upon the mind, the depression deepened, and the susceptibi- lity to the malign influence of infectiou4 germs in- creased. And this distressing process lasted hour after hour, day by day, until the body was buried. No wonder, with all this ghastly factitious environ- ment of gloom intensifying natural grief, and dis- turbing vital functions that reason sometimes tot- tered on her throne. Up then with the blinds, and let in the glad sunshine, to cheer the weary heart, chase away the horrors of the dismal darkness, and send the blood more quickly through the veins! And,in inclement weather,let. all the service be said in the church, and the moutners I e protected at the grave-sid.i by a portable tent. To die was as natural as to be born. All who had influence should Set an example of common-sense and sternly oppose all meretricious, costly, and insanitary funeral observances..

ANOTHER SHIP CANAL -FATALITY AT RUNCORN.

Yesterday afternoon at the Wearer Hotel, Wes- ton Point, Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner for the honour and fee of Halton, held an inquest on the body of Elizabeth Ann Purney, aged four years, daughter of Martha Furney; South-road, Weston Point. The evidence showed that the girl left home about 9-30 on Thursday morning, for the purpose of going to her aunts house, to reach which the little one would have to cross the Manchester Ship Canal railway, and she ap- pears to have wandered up the line. A gatekeeper named John Heywood saw the child on the line, and he noticed that the engine "Kenyon," drawing a train of wagons was coming along at the rate of about four miles an hour. He shouted to the girl but apparently she did not hear him, and continued walking on. Be then ran in front of the engine, and picked up the child but before he could get her clear, the engine knocked her out of his arms, and threw her into the four-foot. The engine and waggons passed over her and Heywood then then found that she had been dreadfully injured about the head. She was taken home, arid Dr. Brunton assistant to Dr. McDougall was quickly in attendance but the child was in a dying condition and she suc- cumbed half an hour later. Heywood. who was also struck by the engine, was bruised and had a narrow escape. The evidence disclosed that children had to cross the lines to get to school, and that there was no protection whatever afforded. The jury strongly expressed the opinion that gates should be fixed at tho various level crossings in the district, and in returning a verdict of "accidental death" made a presentment to that effect.—Mr. Newman (Grundy, Kershaw and Co.) said the Ship Canal Company would be glad to carry out the wishes of the jury if the Daresbury Highway Board would give them the requisite permission.—The Coroner said there was little doubt that the permission would be given,

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse, on Tuesday, under the presidency of Mr. J. Mclntyre. Present the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Niessrs. S. Taylor, T. Lea, A. R. Norman, H. Neild, T. Lewis, J. Stokes, E. Aaron, H. E. Hey wood, John Whitlow, James Whitlow, W. Whitlow, J. Weir, J. Parr, J. Knowles, J. 31. Williams, J. Wilkinson, J. Brandreth, E. Brandreth, Fl Bretherton, I'. Hall, T. Baxter, C. C., R. Wade, W. Wilkinson, J. Ashton (clerk), G. Ashton (deputy clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer), and Mr. Dansey (Local Govern- ment Board Inspector).'

INFECTIOUS DISEASES HOSPITAL. With regard to the prevision of infectious hospital accommodation the Local Government Board stated that when a person suffering from illness, including in- fectious disease, was destitute, it was the duty of the relieving officer to give such relief as was required, and if necessary to give an order for the admission of the patient to a hospital in which he could be properly treated. Where, however, the removal of the patient to a hospital was only required for the purpose of isolation, and the person to I to be removed was not destitute, the Guardians had no duty in the case, and the necessary provision should be made by the Sanitary Authority of the district. The test of the Guardians' duty, in the matter was the destitution of the patient and that would nec:issarily depend upon his being in the actual receipt of pkor relief, but might consist in his being unable to obtain at hi& own cost the requisite medical attendance, nursing, and accommoda- tion. The Board thought that the hospital accommoda- tion required in any locality should be provided by the Sanitary Authority, who should agree with the Guar- dians to receive their pauper infectious cases. The Guardians might enter into an agreement with the Urban Sanitary Authority for the treatment of pauper cases in their hospital, and also with the Rural Sanitary Authority when that Authority had provided a hospital for that district. It was stated that the situation had now changed and there was now no intention on the part of the Guardians to. erect a hospital.—Mr. Brandreth said in the Runcorn Hospital the only inmates who were paid for were paupers. As the. hospital was for the pre- vention of the spread of infection, lie thought no charge should be made at all.—Mr. Dansey said that was the large view some authorities took of the matter.—Mr. Heywood said if the representation of the Commissioners and Guardians could mcet together the matter could be amicably arranged.- —Mr. Lea asked if there had been any cases charged for yet.—The Clerk said there had been one sent in for typhoid fever but not smallpox.— Mr. Lea said he was a member of the Health Commit- tee, and the first consideration of the Committee was to get patients isolated irrespective of cost. The discussion then ceased.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE EDUCATION.

The-Education Department wrote on January 23th, pointing out that the manrers of public elementary schools which are entitled under the Elementary Educa- tion Act, 1892, to continue to charge fees are under no obligation to admit any children without payment of fees. On the other hand the Guardians are entitled un- der section 5 of the Elementary Education Act, 1891, to represent to the Department that there is an insufficient amount of public elementary school accommodation without payment of fees of children over 3 and under 15 years of age, for whom such Guardians have been pay- ing fees, and for whom they desire free school accom- modation, and the E location Department have power to direct such deficiency of free school accommodation to be supplied by the ratepayers in the manner provided by that section.—Mr. Norman said that letter made it clear that if the parents of children chose to send them to a denominational school where fees were charged. the Board could not do otherwise than pay the fees if the parents were unable to do so.—The Clerk concurred.— Mr. Brandreth said he understood from Das speeches of leading politicians that any parent nom(' have free edu- cation'for the asking, and he did not understand why the Guardians should pay fees.—Mr. Taylor said many people had thouqht they could demand free education from Denominational Schools. The letter just read would seem to show that that was not 53.—Mr. Bran- I dreth said in a county district there was frequently only one school, and he contended that a poor parent could demand free education at that school.— Mr. Norman said if the Guardians provideo free schools and the parents still chose to send their children to a denominational school where fees were charged the Guardians would have to pay the fees.—Mr. Dansey : You have power to make the ratepayers provide accom- modation, but you have no power to make parents send their children there.—Mr. Ashton pointed out that it would be an expensive matter to provide a school, and the payment of these fees fell upon Runcorn township : It was not a Union charge.

TIIE REPRESENTATION OF RUNCORN. The Committee appointed to consider the representa- tion of Runcorn on the Board reported that after a long dismission it was proposed by Mr. Norman, seconded by Mr. Brandreth, and resolved unanimously. " That com- paring Runcorn with other townships of the Union, both with regard to rateable value and population, the com- mittee are of opinion that Runcorn is inadequately represented on the Board, and recommend that the Local Government Board be requested to sanction the appoint- ment of three additional Guardians for that township.-- Mr. Heywood, in moving the adoption of this report, said Runcorn was not adequately represented compared with the large towns in other Unions. He also pointed out that Runcorn bad a great many poor, and in regard to relief supervision it would be an advantage to the Board if Runcorn had a larger representation.—Mr. Brandreth seconded the motion, and said Run- corn was not fairly represented, taking into con- sideration the rateable value and population of the place. The rateable value of Runcorn was nearly a quarter of the Union,and according to that should have a quartar of the represeeta don with res- pect to the poor, Runctum had seven-eighths of the poor —(laughter, and "That's no credit to them ")—and from those three points of view,Runcorn was entitled to larger representation.—MrAVeir: I propose that we give them Home Rule. (Laughter.)—Mr. Taylor supported the motion.—Replying to Mr. Whitlow, Mr. Heywood said the committee did not wish to give Runcorn repre- sentatives at the expense of other townships.—Mr.Whi:.- low said the committee knew at the same time that the Local Government Board would not grant any additional Guardians to the union.—Mr. Heywood denied this, and said he hoped the Local Government Board would grant them three additional Guardians.—Mr. Dansey asked if they thought a township like Aston Grange, with only three houses should have a representative.—Mr. W. Wil- kinson : Certainly. (Laughter.)—On the motion being put, thirteen voted for and none against, and the Chair- man declared the resolution passe d unanimously.

FRODSHAM.

THE NEW CONSERVATIVE CLUB.

This club is rapidly approaching completion, and it is anticinated that in the course of another week or so it will b;e ready for occupancy. The structure is plain, but solid, and is built of bricks, with stone and terra cotta dressings. There is an entrance hall with stair- case, large billiard room, and lavatories on the ground floor. Above these are committee or retiring rooms. dressing room and large room, which is being used as a Masonic lodge!for othar purposes. The site, which has been given at a peppercorn rent by Lord Cholmondeley, provides plenty of acccommodation for future extension. The estimated cost of the building is about £l,OOO. The centracts were let to Mr. Littler, builder; Mr. George Cliff, woodwork ; and Mr. F. W. Spencer, plumbing, painting, and decorating. The plans were prepared by Mr. Samuel Davies, architect, under whose supervision the building has been erected. RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. J. Mclntyre presided, and there were present Messrs. H. Neild, T. Lewis, J. Stokes. E. Aaron, H. E. Heywood, mo. Whitlow, Wm. Whitlow. Jas. Whitlow, 3. Weir, J. Parr, J. Knowles, J. W. Williams, J. Wilkinson. J. Brand' retti, E. Bretherton, P. Hall, T. Baiter, C.C., R. Wade, W. Wilkinson, J. Ashton (clerk), G. Ashton (deputy clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer) and Mr. Dansey (Local Government Board Inspector.)

WATER SUPPLY TO ASTON, DUTTON, lie. The Local Uovernment Board, adverting to the letter of the Rural Sanitary Authority, of the 4th December, upon the subject of the supply of water to certain pre- mises in the township of Aston, Dutton, and Preston-on- the-Hill, the rural district of Vie Runcorn Union wrote stating that from the information furnished to the Board it did not appear that a supply could be provided to the houses in question, within the limit of cost prescribed by section 3 of the Public Health (Water) Act, 1878, and that they therefore propose to issue an order cancelling the requirement of the Rural Sanitary Authority as to the provision of a supply to the houses in question. The Board requested that they might be furnished with any observations which the Authority might wish to offer as tc the suanestion that a supply should be furnished from the Workhouse well.—A committee consisting of Messrs. Baxter, Wilkinson, W. Whitlow, J. Stokes, and H. E. Heywood, was appointed to deal with the ques- tion.

PAYMENT FROM THE COUNTY COUNCIL. Mr. James Williams, county accountant and auditor, notified the Authority that a cheque for £l3O on account of the salaries of the sanitary officers of the Union would be drawn after the County Council meeting on the 9th inst. ' THE SEWERAGE OF HELSBY.—A PROTEST. Under date January 30th, Mr. W. Farish, of Hillside Villa, Helsby, Cheshire, wrote stating that " our excel- ent guardian,Mr. Brandreth," had supplied him with a ropy of the report of tne Inspector sent the Authority by the Local Government Board respecting the sewering of Flelsby. He entirely objected to the carrying out of ally such extravagant scheme. To give effect to the In- pector's suggestions would involve a cost of many thou- sands of pounds in the first instance, and a permanent annual outlay upon the ratepayers for all time to come. If Helsby were an abnormally unhealthy village,or if any portion of the inhabitants were suffering seriously in their health, or were even likely to suffer through what was called defective drainage, it would become the im- parative duty of the authorities to rectify it with all pos- sible despatch and almost regardless of expense. But seeing that none of these conditions existed and there was no immediate danger of them arising, it would be worse than folly to launch such a burden upon the shoul- ders of many who at this particular time would be unable to bear it. At least it was an inconsiderate move- ment and reflected little credit upon either the wisdom or the forethought fat those who set it on foot. If the town- ship had to be put to great expense to satisfy the whim or allay the fears of nervous, fidgetty people, then the soonerimportant changes were !Ade the better.— Mr. Brandreth suggested that the letter should be pub- lished in the newspapers,and Mr. Parr said it was a very sensible letter. The Telegraph Manufacturing Company, Ld., wrote draw ing the attention of the Authority to the defective condition of the outfall of drainage from the Co.'s cottages at Helsby.—Mr. Braudreth said an alteration was needed. The communication was referred to the Helsby Committee.

THE SMALLPDX EPIDEMIC.-INFECTING A NEIGHBOUR- HOOD.

Mr. Williams, chairman of the Infectious Diseases Hospital Committee, said that acting under the autho- rity conferred upon Dr. Adams and himself, he had to report that with the very valuable and active assistance of the Ship Canal Company, Mr. Jackson. the contrac- tor,their officeis and men,they had succeeded in provid- ing and equipping a very sightly and comfortable tem- porary hospital near to Moore, capable of receiving 12 male and 8 female patients, 2 nurses, and one Male at- tendant. The buildings comprised male and female wards, nurses' room, kitchen, bath, man's bedroom,and store room in the main building; with wash-hause, coal store,coach-house.and mortuary in a detached building. The furnishing had been completed for 15 patients and 15 attendants, but Dr. Adams found it absolutely neces- sary to provide 7 additional beds and bedding. There being no time for seeking contracts for the work or furniture, they had as far as convenient placed the busi- ness with those having contracts for supplying Dutton Workhouse. There would be fifteen patients in the hos- pital that day. The Canal Company and Mr. Jackson had not yet given any account of the Authority's share of the expenses incurred, but they were necessarily con- siderable, some fifteen or more men having been em- ployed daily and many nights for the last fourteen days. It might not he out of place if he, as chairman of the committee, thankfully acknowledged the very consider- able expenditure of personal attention and time given to this business by Dr. Adams and Mr. Farrington, whilst to the Ship Canal officials and men they owed their most thankful acknowledgments. Every officer and man had worked with a willing heartiness which had been worthy of their sincere admiration and gratitude.— Mr. Heywood moved the adoption of the report, and said their thanks were due to Mr. Williams as well as the other people mentioned: he had been most indefatig- able. Lie also proposed that Mr. H. William, be paid his out of pocket expenses.—Mr.W. Wilkinson seconded the motion.—Mr. Dansey said credit was reflected upon the Board for their wisdom in choosing such representa- tives. The motion was carried.

Dr. Adams reported that since the last meeting six fresh cases of smallpox had occurred in Stockton Heath, one at Weston village, and 12 at the huts,Acton Grange. One patient died cm Monday from malignant smallpox. No fresh cases were reported from Frodsham.—ln an- swer to Mr. W. Whitlow the Medical Officer said three of the patimts had been re-vaccinated. Three days after being re-vaccinated they developed the disease, which was of an exceedingly mild type, showing that they had had the disease some live days before they were re-vaccinated.—Mr. Weir said he had heard of a man at Stockton Heath suffering from smallpox, who had threatened, because he was stopped from working, that he would infect all the neighbourhood, and he was walking about the place daily.—Mr. De Prez, relieving officer, said he could produce evidence as to the man's movements. He had made several journeys to the post office on some pretext or another until the post- master had threatened to have him locked up. The man had also travelled to Warrington in the bus on several occasions too, and he had frequented two of the public houses in the district.—Mr. Dansey said the man ought to be made an example of, and Mr. Heywood moved that Mr. Farrington, the nuisance inspector take steps to prosecute the man before the magistrates.—Mr. Parr seconded the motion, which was carried unanim- ously.—A long discussion took place as to the provision of appliances for disinfecting 'bedding and clothing and the appointment of a medical !operintendent at the hospital,and eventually the subjects were referred to the , committee, who it was arranged shonld meet at the ' Lion Hotel, Warrington, at 5-30 on Wednesday even- ing.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The first meeting of the newly elected Board was held on Tuesday night. Present Messrs. J. Prit- chard, Dr. AlcDougall, T. Handley, S. Taylor, E. !raison, G. Moss (clerk).—On the motion of Dr. McDougall, seconded by Mr. Handley, Mr. Pritchard was appointed chairman for the ensuing three years.—Dr. McDougall was elected vice-chairman. The School Attendance Committee was constituted of the Rev. Canon Wood, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Mes:rs. S. Taylor, and Thomas Handley, and Dr. McDougall ; Messrs. E. liaison and T. Handley were appointed the School Man- agement Committee. The Chairman is an ex-officio mem- ber of the Committees.—The attendance officers reported an improved average attendance compared with the corresponding month last year. There was an increase in the number on the rolls of 123 and an increase in average attendance of 234.—The Clerk attributed the improved attendance to the absence of epidemic sickness amongst children. The percentage of attendance was ; corresponding month last year 76; increase 4.—The I resignation of Mr. Jarvis, assistant master at Greenway- road boys' school, was accepted —An application from Mr. E. --- COMIC (VERA- The famous Burns-Crotty Opera Company will visit St. Helens next week and produce the charm- ing comic operas of " Cinderella " and "The Bohemian Girl." Mr. Wallace Bevil!, the enter- prising proprietor of the Theatre Royal and Opera House., has arranged for the visit of the talented company with all their gorgeous costumes, elaborate scenery, and mechanical effects, and the engagement should cer- tainly prove the treat of a high class histrionic season. The accomplished artistes Madame Georgina Burns and Mr. Leslie Crotty, whose names are familiar to all in- terested an the musical world, will pourtray the leading characters in the productions and they will be supported by some of the finest performers now touring the coun- try. Details of the performances appear in our adver- tising columns.

1 WARRINGTON COUNTY COURT.

THITIZSDAY.—Before his Honour, Judge Wynne

Ffoulkes.

I THE CLAIM FOIL A HUNDRED Genss.—The adjourned , case in which Thomas Morris, poulterer, Heath Side, , Warrington, claimed £36 16s. 7d., from Samuel John Francis, fish dealer and poulterer, Widnes, for 104 geese , supplied, was proceeded with. Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, and Dr. Cominse instructed by Mr. Knowles, of Widnes, for the defendant.—Dr. O'Keeffe, medical officer of health for Widnes, was again placed in the box. He deposed to examining 90 geese, all at the defendant's shop, except six he examined at the Police Station, all of the 90 being bad. The smell made him sick, so that he had to examine ;Dem on different occa- sions. They smelt so badly that the smell would be , easily discernible by any ordinary person. The geese were in an advanced state of decomposition, and must have smelt for a fortnight. They were packed tightly in hampers. There were no other geese on the defend- ant's premises that witness could see, unless they were upstairs. The geese were in an advanced stage of de- composition. Anyone could have smelt them on the Wednesday, the 21st. The geese were fine geese in ap- pearance and weight.—Mrs. Francis, wife of the defend- ant, deposed to purchasing the geese from the plaintiff on the 22nd of December, and taking them home packed in hampers in a cart. She di el not have them opened or handle them before coming away. She said they were rather small for her customers. Mrs. Morris charged her 80. a lb., a id. a lb. more than usual, and guaranteed them good. Witness supplied 14 to her cus- tomers on the 22 nd ,these having been paid for in advance. A Mrs. Summers brought one back on the 23rd, and said it was bad. Witness gave her another, and she brought it back, saying her husband would kill her if she kept it. It was very bad. The most of the 14 geese were brought back. One or two her customers kept, but they were so bad that they had to throw them away, and she had to allow for them. She examined the re- mainder of the geese she had bought from the defend- ant, and found them all bad. In reply to Mr. Browne she said she only dealt in poultry at Christmas.—By the Judge: She detected no smell when they were packed. —James McCreary, poulterer, of Widnes, said his ex- perience was that if the geese were drawn and scalded on Wednesday, the 21st December, as stated, the smell would be taken away for the time being.—Walter Lowe, sanitary inspector for the borough of Widnes, deposed to his assisting Dr. O'Keeffe to examine the geese, and said they were unfit for the food of man.—By Mr. Browne: They did not tell him the geese had come from Liverpool.—Mary O'Connor, of Widnes, also gave evid- ence. She said she was present with Mrs. Francis when she bought the geese. None of them were opened, and Mrs. Francis did not touch them. Witness and Mrs. Francis came home together. 'I he geese were laid out on her table in the kitcten.There were no geese obtained by the defendant anywhere es.—Samuel John Francis, the defendant, was then called. He dep-ised to order- ing the geese from the plaintiff's wife. He had then no other geese in his shop, nor did he •ibtain others until he ascertained that those he had bought were unfit for food. He charged a pound more than usual because geese were so scarce. Mr. Browne cross-examined this witness as to the telegram he sent the plaintiff about the geese and asked him if it was his telegram, on which he replied that he did not write it, he handed it in. (Laughter.) He further, in reply to Mr. Browne, said be had not told anyone that he did not care how the case went as he had made him- self safe by turning everything over. The shop was in his wife's name not his.—Mrs. Sumner, of Widnes, deposed that on Thursday, the 22ad of December, she purchased a goose from the defendant and on the Friday discovered it to be bad, and brought it back, and got another, which also proved bad. They were not fit for Christians to have in their houses.—Margaret Timnperley, of Widnes, gave similar evidence as to a goose she bought being bad.—Alice Bradshaw said she bought a. goose on Christmas Eve at the stall of Mr. Morris at i Warrington, and found when she got home that t was bad. She took it back, and a little girl gave her the money back. If Mr. Sheldon said there was not a single goose sold by the plaintiff returned, that was not true. —By Mr. Browne : She did not take back the goose to have it changed for's. duck. She had never said to Mrs. Jennings she would go far any side that vaid her best.— Dr.Comins addressed the court for the defence. He said it was indisputable that the geese condemned were unfit for human food, and that if so unfit as they were pro- nounced to have been when condemned, they were unfit on the day they were bought. Oaly one point remained, and that was, were the geese condemned. those sold by Mr. Morris to the defendant ? If that was preyed the plaintiff was guilty of a gross fraud.—The Judge: There is no defence of fraud. —Dr. Coming submitted that the evidence showed this.—The Judge: But you could file a counter claim for fraud. —Dr. Comins : That is a matter for future consideration. Something may be done bye and bye.—The Judge said there was no counter claim for damage for fraud.—Mr. Browne : Although it is threa- tened in the original letter.—Dr. Comins said the claim might be beyond the jurisdiction.—Mr. Browne: Yoa can make your counter claim for any amount so long as the original claim is £so.—Dr. Combos contended th. the pretence that the geese condemnedlwere not those sold by the plaintiff had utterly failed.—Mr. Browne said this was practically an undefended action, as the only defence that could have been set up was that the plaintiff had given a guarantee or a warranty, but the evidence of the defendant himself proved that there was none. He said the plaintiff charged per lb. more than usual because geese were so scarce, but not a word was said about guarantee. Mr. Browne called attention to the Lict that Dr. O'Keeffe said the geese were all packed tightly in baskets in sixes, and the plaintiff's evidence was to the like effect. Mrs. Francis said there were 16 hampers with only six each in them—that would make 96—and yet the doctor said they were tightly packed. This went to show that the geese he examined were other geese. The evidence of those experienced in poultry that no smell was perceptible when the geese were bought proved that they were good.—The Judge in summing up said there was no warranty of the geese, and the purchaser having ample opportunity of examin- ing them when purchasing, took them at her own risk, but he expressed the opinion that she did examine some of them, and that all of them were perfectly good when taken. He commented on the fact that one witness said the geese were laid out on Mr. Francis's table while Dr. O'Keeffe said he found them in hampers, went to show that those condemned were not those bought of the plaintiff. His Honour gave a verdict for the plaintiff for the full amount claimed, with costs.

PENKETH.

INFANTS' CONCERT. On Friday evening last a very pleasing concert was given by the infants of the Penketh ‘Vesleyan School. The concert consisted of musical drill, songs, recitations, and concerted action pieces by the children. The programme was performed in a very clever and pleasing manner, the smart render- ing of the various pieces giving the highest pleasure to the crowded audience. In the second part of the programme a miniature play," Fairyland,' was rendered by the infants, various well-known fairy personages being depicted. The entire charge was taken by Mrs. Wilson, head mistress, assisted by the teachers. Miss E. 11. Watson ably accompanied on the pianoforte. MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. The weekly meeting of the above was lijld in the Wesleyan School on Wednesday night, Mr. C. E. Parker in the chair. In consequence of the essayist for the evening being unable to attend, the teachers and senior scholars from the Friends' Kthool kindly filled up the evening. The programme was very attractive and well rendered. The following are those who took part :—Mrs. Pollard. Messrs. Theo- bald, Reynolds, Linney, and Pollard; Masters T. Yowl, Darbyshire, Spencer, and Sudlow. Mr. R. Garnett proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the performers, and welcomed Mr. Pollard (the new headmaster) to the Association. Mr. J. Peers seconded, and the meeting closed with the National Anthem.

At the Woodhouse Moor Wesleyan Chapel, ea - ingly, Leeds, on Thursday, the wedding of Mr. R. E. Garnett, of Hall Nook, Peuketh, eldest son of Mr. R. Garnett and Sons, Sankey-st. and Penketh. Mr. Garnett is much esteemed by those who know him and on all sides, best wishes for the future of himself and his bride are expressed.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DISCHARGING FIREWORKS IN A KEYHOLE. Thomas Massom,labourer,l4l,Knutsford-road ; George Hamblett, labourer, 148, Keutsford-road ; and John Fairhurst, 9, Barry-street, were charged with discharging a-firework in Knutsford-road, about half-past ten on the night of the 31st ult.—The constable who appeared in the case said the firework was fired from the street through the keyhole of a house door.—Fairhurst pleaded guilty and said the other defendants had nothing to dP with the matter, and they denied their guilt, saying that they were with him at the time, but remonstrated with Aim.—The Rev. E. T. Reed, pastorof Emmanuel Church, said the defendants were teachers in his Sunday sclaool, and very respectable, well-conducted young men, and he pleaded in their favour.—Fairhurst was fined 2s. 6d. and costs, and Alderman Harrison (the chairman) told him the practice was a most dangerous one. Someone in the house might have had heart disease and been so startled by the report as to die. Masson and Hamblett I were dismissed. AN ODD (I) THEFT OF BOOTS. At the Warrington Police Court, on Saturday, James Savage Shaw, a respectable-looking man, who lives at 26, Norman-st., was charged with having stolen two odd boots from the shop of William Leslie, Mersey-st., on Friday night. The prisoner pleaded not guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that the prosecutor was a general dealer, and occupied a shop in Mersey-at. The prisoner was employed as a tanner at Messrs. Reynolds's works. About nine o'clock, he and another man went into the shop and stopped there for some time talking TO Mr. Leslie. They afterwards went out, without having made any purchase. The prisoner came back to the shop about half an hour later with a woman who wanted to exchange a pair of boots. After doing this the woman went out and the prisoner followed. When go- ing past the shop window Mrs. Leslie noticed a pair of boots in his hands. She went after him and asked him what he was doing with the boots. He replied, " Why, haven't I paid for them ?" Mrs. Leslie told him that he had not, and that she had to deal with far "too many of his kind." P.C. Turner was informed of the occur- rence, and he proceeded to the shop. At eleven o'clock the same evening Detective Monaghan went to the pri- soner's house and there saw him. Shaw then told him that he knew nothing about the boots. lie was brought to the Police Station and identified by Mrs. Leslie. When ' charged with the theft of the boots he replied, "That's what you say." The prisoner said he did not remember taking the hoots. On Friday night he went to a public. house with a number of friends, where a new hand was paying his "footing," as it was called. He came out intending to go to the theatre where his wife was, when he met a relative of his, and as she was going to Mrs. Leslie's he said he would go with her. lie did not re- member taking any boots, or if he had wanted them what good would an odd pair have been to him? He did not attempt to conceal the boots in any way.—Fined ss. and costs. DEFECTIVE WEIGHTS AND MEASURES. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday, John Wainwright, whose premises are at Bridge Foot, and who resides at No. 11, Wilderspool Causeway, was ' charged by Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk,with using on the 30th ult., a pair of scales that were unjust.—The Town Clerk said the scales, which were used for weighing tobacco only, were right in themselves, but there were two pieces of paper under them which made them 11 drachms against the purchaser. He had known Mr. Wainwright all his life as a respectable man.—The Market Inspector's assistant deposed that he drew the defendant's attention to the pieces of paper, and he said he did not know how two pieces of paper got under the scales, but some children had been playing with them, Witness took the scales to Mr. Place.—The defendant's wife said some children were in with hers and they had been playing with the scales. She chastised them on Sunday for doing so, and never I thought they had put anything underneath them. They ' were playing at hiding things and finding them. The Inspector went next door, and she knew this, and could have removed the paper in his absence had she known it was there.—The Town Clerk said there was something in tha:rernark.—Alderman Harrison ,who was presiding, remarked that the fact of the scales being unjust was an offence in itself, no matter what was the cause; and the defendant was fined 55.—John Daley, 63, Old-road, was charged by the Town Clerk with having five weights and a weighing machine out of order.—The Town Clerk said the machine was of an ounce against the seller. caused by one of the arms being longer than the other. —The Chairman remarked that it was an offence whether the scales were against the seller or the purchaser.—Mr. Whittle : Just the same.—Mr. Place's assistant said the Town Clerk's statements were true.—Mr. Place said he had condemned the weights some time since and taken the lead out of them telling the defendant he must not use them, but he had gone on doing so.—He was fined 10s, and costs in each case, 3ss. in all.

A TERROR TO THE COMIMNITY. Edward Griffin, jun., was charged with stealing a piece of meat,value 45., from the shop of Walter Charles butcher, winwick-st.,and James Griffin, Edward Griffin sen., and Jane Dooney, were charged with. assaulting Constable Amson on the previous night whilst in the execution of his duty.—James Griffin pleaded guilty and the others not guilty. Constable Amson said that about six o'clock on Monday night, from what Mr. Charles told him, he went to Town's-end. He saw the prisoners in company with another man, and asked them to go with him to the shop. They went quietly, and as the witnesses who saw the theft were not there, he said to ltdward Griffin, jun.." All right Mr. Griffin, go away." His father shouted," I will summon you for defamation of my son's character." His daughter and sister of the other prisoners, Mrs. Dooney, behaved in a very violent manner, and Edward Griffin, jun., tried to rush past him into the shop, exclaiming, "I will give him saying I stole the beef." He struck witness slightly, but he (witness) took no notice of it, and then James struck him on the other side. He strgggled with them, and Edward struck him again, giving him a black eye. Witness tried to take him into custody, when James got hold of him by the legs, and threw him backwards through the open window of the shop. He was knocked down twice and kicked by Mrs. Dooney on the back of the head. He eventually got up and drew his staff, but was obliged to let the prisoners go as there wag; such a crowd round them. He afterwards went in search.of Edward Griffin, jun., and found him in the 'Soldiers Return," Pinner's-brow. When charged with stealing the beef he said he knew nothing about it, but did not make any answer with regard to the assault. James was brought into the police station afterwards, and in regard to the assault he said" All right," Edward Griffin. sen., said " I will say nothing to you. but I will speak to your betters."—Edward Griffin, jun. leas sentenced to one month's hard labour for stealing the meat, and two months. for the assault on the con- stable ; James was committFd for two months, and Edward Griffin, sen., was discharged with a caution. The female prisoner, Jane Rooney, was fined 10s. including costs. The Chief Constable said the witnesses came very reluctantly, for many were afraid of the Griffins, ivho were increasing, and multiplying, and they were now a very large family.

A DISORDERLY PAUPER.—Mary SCally, a pauper at the Union Workhouse, was charged with having been disorderly in the workhouse on Friday night at a quar- ter to eight.—Defendant pleaded not guilty.—Mr. Shearer, relieving officer, said that the defendant took her discharge on Friday morning, and came back in the evening. The porter found that she was the worse for drink, and he could not keep her quiet. It was not the first time the woman had done such a thing. She wanted them to let her go, and to leave her four child- ren in the Workhouse. Here Potts ran strongly, but could not force a passage. The visitors continued to press severely, owing to the very fine display of the forwards, audit was mainly owing to their superb play that the warfare was contested at , least 60 out of the 70 minutes in Oldham territory. Final—Warrington, 2-1-4 to 0-0-0. .

RUNCORN GREAT CHARITY MATCIL—the "Liverpool Mercury" thus refers to the forthcoming match at Liverpool for the Davies fund and the benefit of the club funds :—lt is a pleasure to be able to announce the de- finite settlement of the Runcorn and Swinton match. Apart from the interest in the encounter naturally aris- ing from the peculiar circumstances which have led up to the fixture—the severe financial handicapping of Run- corn, playing in a town of small "gate" power, and subject to heavy travelling expenses—the match offers unique features that will doubtless attract a peculiarly mixed attendance at Goedison Park. To see the cham- pions of Lancashire and the champions of Cheshire on neutral territory, and in the very stronghold of the rival game, is like the worship of Mercury in the Temple of Apollo. No doubt the novelty will attract a goodly sprinkling of the Everton devotees, curious to see a game about which most of them know very little, and willing to have it brought to their doors and expounded by adepts. Nor is the Rugby-seeing following likely to miss an opportunity of seeing what is undoubtedly the first football ground in the country. The Everton man- 'gement are already notorious for their generosity in arity and benefit matches, and this can do little to add to their credit, so well is it already established. But it is no usual courtesy to grant association ground for the help of a Rugby club, free of all cost, as the Everton committee are doing, and this fact deserves to be well known. We wish this unique match every success, and a heavy gate for Runcorn.

ANTI-OPIUM TRAFFIC MEETING, AT

WAItRINGTON.

APPALLING DISCLOSURES. On Monday evening a largely attended meeting was held in the Friends' Meeting House, Butter- market-sttreet, Warrington, in support of the move- ment for the abolition of the opium traffic in India and China. Mr. J. Rymer Young presided, and was supported by the Revs. J. Yonge and E. T. Reed, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Miss Soonderbai Power, a Christian lady from Bombay, Mr. Alexander Lang- man, of the China Inland Mission, and Mr. John Darbyshire. • Mr. Hormss read a letter from the Rector of Warring- ton (the Rev. F. W. Willis) regretting his inability to be present, having to attend that night the first meeting of the temperance society in connection with his church. Miss Holmes also read letters from Miss Dutton of I Moore, the Rev. James Warnock, and Councillor H. Roberts expressing regret at being prevented from attending. The Crukuushas in opening the meeting said he sup- posed all of them, in a general sort of way, were aware that the habit of opium smoking was exceedingly harm- ful and demoralising ; and they all had in a hazy sort of way a general impression as to the matter of the opium trade, that a long time ago a monstrous iniquity was perpetrated by the Government of the day, an iniquity they were apt to forget had been perpetuated down to the present day. But as a matter of fact they were not gifren to entertain in their minds too much subjects which were disagreeable and which reflected discredit upon themselves or their nation. It was wise now and then that they should be reminded by people who had been in India and China of the evils of this opium traffic, and that new and again their minds should be excited on the question. Miss SOoNDERBAI PUNTER then rose, amidst much applause, to speak. She referred, in the first place, to the discouragement she had met with from her friends in India as to her leaving that country on her present mission of agitating against the opium traffic, because of danger to her health in coming to the climate of Eng- land at the present season trent her own sunny country. She then proceeded to speak on the extensive use of opium in India, and said Hindoo mothers generally were in the habit of giving it to their babies to quiet thera, and hundreds of them died through being overdosed. In India high caste widows had to suffer a great deal. They were treated just like animals and slaves. The mother-in-laws of some of them were very cruel to them, and when they got tired of their lives they just went to the market, bought opium, and poisoned themselves. Opium was a great temptation to the Flindoos, because I they could go into the fruit or vegetable market and see la large stall of opium, and it was very cheap. Yoe could send a girl eight years old, and she would buy 360 grains I without any difficulty. There were in India lets of troublesome rats, and when people wanted to buy arsenic to poison them what a fuss was made about it! They had to give their name and address and all sorts of things. But opium poisoned just like arsenic, and you I could get any quantity of it without trouble. They could get poison to -kill a man, but, not to kill rats. (Applause.) There were Hindoo women who had said to her that she must tell I the English people that their children their young men were dying, that India was ruined through this opium, and that if the English would stop this growth of the I poppy there, the Hindoos would think they were their. gods, and worship them. But then, it was said, their Christian Government wanted money, and the poor liindoo people said if they wanted money let them send their soldiers and take their houses and land and all they had and they would work hard to support themselves, )f only they might be delivered from the opium, by which they lost both their money and their strength, and were ruined for ever. The people of India said to her she I must tell the English that if they would but relieve them ' from the curse of opium' they would take the skins off their bodies and make shoes for them, to show their gratitude. They said if only this opium traffic could be stopped they might send their soldiers and kill all the wives and children of opium smokers. They said, speaking of the missionaries being sent to convert them, "Go and convert your Christian Government fint."Some said " We do not want your Jesus, your religion ; the Christian people have ruined us." When Christians saw their dear Saviour disgraced by Christian people in this manner, wriat could they say to the Hindoos ? Unless this great hindrance of the opium traffic were removed they would never see India and China Christian though they sent thousands of missionaries and spent any amount of money. If the revenue derived from the traffic was the price of blomd, Christ would never bless it. (Applause.) The speaker then gave a glimpse of some of the horrors of an Indian opium den, and des- cribed the arts used by opium merchants and others, such as putting the drug in tea or coffee or sweets, to imbue the people with a taste for it. She complained that while the " Times " published articles and inserted letters in favour of the opium traffic, it refused to in- sert communications from the anti-opium side. The " Times," she said, declared opium to be a harmless drug. If so, why was it labelled poison in England, and why had they said in the House of Commons that they would try all they could to restrict its consumption.? Some said it was food—a necessary of life. Why, then, was it so,heavily taxed? (Applause.) Sonsesaid it was very good for malarial fever. She never heard in her life of anybody using opium for fever. They always in India took quinine, or an Indian medicine made from a certain herb, for fever. If opium was good for fever let it be with the doctors and chemists, not with the mer- chants. Supposing she wrote an article in the " Times " stating that arsenic was good for influenza, and rheumatic gout. She would be called insane, and that was what they said in India about the writers of the " Times, " on opium. (Laughter.) Why did not the English people push this opium trade in Russia ? No, they dared not try it. They tried to kill the weak ones, and then called themselves a strong nation, but they in India did not call them so, whatever they called themselves- A strong nation protected the weak. Miss Power mentioned that 50 years ago there was not an opium smoker in Burnish, and now, under British gov- ernment, there were thousands of opium dens in that country. If England, she said, could not rule India wisely and justly the best plan would be to give it up, rather than ruin the people. (Loud cries of " hear, hear.") Miss Power expressed the opinion that her cause would triumph because the Lord was on its side. He had heard the millions of cries of Chinese and Indians and Burmese for justice, and He was stirring up the English people to work for the cause. The speaker resumed her seat amidst considerable applause. Mr. LA,Nomes then gave some harrowing accounts, of his experiences of the results of the opium trade in China and described the iniquitous and barbarous wars by which Great Britain had enforced the traffic upon China, one of these wars having been denounced by Lord Elgin himself, who had charge of it, as a cruel tramp- ling under foot of an ancient civilization for the most selfish purposes. As an instance of the infatuation for opium he mentioned the Case of the secretary to the governor of a Chinese city in which he had lived selling his wife and four children to obtain means to purchase

the drug. The Rev. J. TONGS proposed the following resolution : —" That this public meeting held at Warrington this Bth day of February 1893, deeply regrets that the traffic in opium is still being carried on by the Anglo-Indian Government, notwithstanding its condemnation by the House of Commons in April 1891. They regard the manufacture and the sale of opium for the purposes of sensual indulgence as a sin against God, and urge the Government to abandon this traffic except for medicinal purposes." Mr. Yonge also moved that copies of the resolution be sent to Lord Kimberley, Secretary of State, for India, the Right Hon. W. E. Gladstone, and the member for the borough. He said that meeting had had something in the shape of a new sensation that night. They were all familiar with missionaries sent from this country to India,but to-night they had listened to a missionary from India,an Indian lady who in a most heroic and Christian spirit had come across to this country in a very unseasonable time of the year to plead the cause of her sisters and brothers in India. He thought when those pmsent reflected upon this fact they would feel a pathos in it. To him it was intensely pathetic that this Christian lady should come to them who had been long in Christ in a sense, to ask them to do an act of simple justice to that vast country which they must never forget was a part of our own empire. The cause she came to advocate that night was one of simple justice and righteousness, and it ought to excite sympathy net only through that meeting, but right through Warrington. The Rev. E. T. REED seconded the proposition. He said it seemed to him a shame and a disgrace that any such resolution should have to be sent to Parliament. He thought the thanks of the entire Christian commu- nity of Warrington were duo to the members Of the Society of Friends for arranging that meeting. He earnestly hoped, with Mr. Yonge, that there would be a great stirring up among the inhabitants of Warrington on this question. The motion was heartily carried, and a vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman, on the motion of Mr. HoLmEs, seconded by Mr. W.ROBERTS, and supported by Mr. DARBYSHIRE, and the Chairman's reply closed the proceedings. A collection was made in aid of the objects of the meet- ing.

Two months had nearly elapsed since Warrington bad indulged in a League match, their latest effort resulting in the decisive defeat

of Wigan, but on the other hand Oldham had got well nigh through half their programme,having, played elevn matches but with indifferent suecess. The homesters turned out as advertised, but Oldham substituted Lees for Thomas, the change as it turned out proving very effective. ASSOCIATION.

RUNCORN ASSOCIATIOV;7I;R-00-911. AM " A " TEAM.— Played at Runcorn on Saturday. The Runcorn team was composed as follows :—Mainwaring, goal ; Hughes and Fisher, full backs ; Bate, A. Limb, and the Rev. G. H. Kingston, half backs ; Limb, Wright, Pye, Hampeon and Blower, forwards. For Fredsham there turned out— Nicholson, goal ; Waddington and White, full backs; Wood, Linaker, and Hazard, half backs ;Jones, T. Jones Linaker, H. Linaker, and Bartle, forwards. The game opened greatly in favour of the home team, who during a short snell of pressing scored two points within ten minutes from the start. Pye being instrumental in their attainment. The visitors played pluckily, their backs robbing the home forwards again and again. Rushing down the field their forwards combined effectively, and Linaker notched the first goal for his side amid cheers. Some pretty passing was now seen among the Runcorn forwards, the sight of which gladdened the heart of the spectators, who now hope that the team have got settled down, and that sunny days are in store for them their prior weakness being due to a great extent to their absention from combination. Nothing further was scored at half time, Runcorn crossing over with a goal , to their credit. From the kick-off Runcorn, with the wind at their backs, pre,sed, and it was soon seen that the game was entirely in the hands of the hemesters, who finally piled on six mere goals, through Pye, Limb, and Blainer respectively, Pye playing an exceptianally pretty game. Frodsham scored one more point, Lineker securing and sending in a long shot entirely out of the reach of Mainwaring, who had been indulging in some training exercise among the half backs. Final score read Runcorn 8, Frodsham 2.

Three miners, named John Oliver, Joseph Jones, and Aaron Crew, lost their lives by lamentable accident on Wednesday night at Messrs. Cochrane's colliery, near Brierley Hill. Staffordshire. They were being drawn up a shaft ene hundred and fifteen yards deep, and when near the top the rope slipped from the drum of the engine, and the cage they were in decended with fearful velocity, crashing through the sump. The men fell into deep water, and their bodies were not recovered for several hours. The WTI, who lived near Dudley, were all married, and leave families.
