THAT PUBLIC HALL AGEN.

Caun't sum owd Earlestown chum send me a line, Tellin' me summut aw connot divine; Wen they're to oppen that three-corner't den ; Which, wen yo look at it, causes yo t' sken ! Look wheer thay'n plantet that noble front door, Just in a spot wheer the property's poor; Strangers'all ax, wi' a smile er a sneer, " Is it a workheause theyn getten deawn' here ?" Look at them windows—say, am aw not reet, Sayin' they're plann'el just fer darkenin' th' leet Oh ! wot a savin', wen this cums to pass— Mornin', noon, net, thay'll be forced to bran gas ! This, that economy which, it war sed, Coom into ev'ry Commissioner's yed ; Seemin' aw reet till yo get a surprise, Wen yo find eawt heaw yore taxes 'till rise ! Then ther'll be ruckshuns,but then its to late, Yo, aw alung, hey invited yore fate; Lettin' a thickyed er two work ther wost, Wile yo sit quiet, ne'er ceauntin' the cost. Five theausand peaund war the figger fost fix'd, But befoor lung this ameaunt geet weel mix'd ; Aye, afoor ever thay finish that" fad," Other five theausand 'ult go to the bad 1 Wot'll it cost when they cum t' decorate Rooms wheer th' Commissioners gether i' state? Pictures thay'll want to look cheerful an' nice, Sayin', in sperrit, "to h-11 wi' the price 1". But, shun aw portraits I—One, not far off, Gies yo a twist i' yore ribs, er a cough! Then, by aw means, make it ever so clear, Yole help no owd woman's vanity theer ! Still yo met hey aw the patterns on th' walls, Summut i'th' shape ov a gentlemon's " smalls ;" Summut to illustrate th' cheermon's speeches, Wen he remark't oath' surveyor's new breeches Wen th' Hall is oppen't, yole want it a name, Summut to put on the greyt scroll o' fame; Well, here it is I—Yo caunt make it to plain— " The Viaduct's folly I The Nyowtoner's Bane 1" TEM 0' PEGS. Rose Bonk, February 10th, 1893,

ST HELENS GLASS BOTTLE HANDS IN CONFERENCE.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
• FOREIGN WAGES CONTRASTED WITH LANCASHIRE. NO STRUGGLE, FOR THIRTY-FIVE YEARS.

A meeting of the St. Helens District of the Lan- cashire Bottle Makers' Trade Protection Association was held on Saturday afternoor., in the Circus, North-road, St. Helens, about 300 members being present. The present state of the trade and the strained relations between employer and employed which now prevail, were discussed, and it was de- cided, after private proceedings lasting five hours, to hold if possible another national conference, to be Leld at St. Helens next Wednesday,and the same secretary was instructed to write to Mr. A. Green- wood, the international secretary, asking him to call to- gether delegates. In conversation with one of the St. Helens workmen on Saturday a press representative was informed that the Lancashire workmen enjoyed greater privileges than any other district in the trade. They work under wlat is known as the half wage system. If , a man goes to work and the furnaces are out of repair, ' or the metal is bad an6l he can't start, the employers pay him half a day's wages. Should he start work and blow only one dozen bottles and be stopped on account of bad metal,the masters are bound to pay him for full journey. These and other privileges the Yorkshire workmen do not enjoy, and he urged on behalf of the Lancashire men, who are averse to a strike, that while they work under the 1893 rates they earn more money than the Yorkshire men do on the 1892. In the St. Helens district the men make dark and light bottles. It is on the dark , metal that the foreign competition i keenly felt,the St. Helens masters stating that they can buy dark foreign bottles cheaper than they can manufacture them. This was explained by the very low wages under which the foreigner worked, and as an instance he said that at Oldenburg, in Germany, where the highest wages were paid, the average per week paid was only £1 and free rent, for sixty-five hours. The men worked seven hours en a Sunday, and there was talk of further reductions taking place in Germany. In Liverpool one large firm had ceased trad- ing with St. Helens manufacturers, because the foreign masters could offer bottles at 6d. per gross less. There was a larger percentage of breakage on the foreign bot- tles, but the only question was the price. Referring to the threatened strike at St. Helens, the workman said that he was afraid, considering the present attitude of both the men and the masters., a strike would take place and it would be a hard fight. Personally, he was averse to strikes, as he considered that the nest interests of both masters and men were served by an amicable arrangement. In St. Helens it was thirty-five years since a strike took place, and that lasted three months. Since then the workmen had been on the most amicable terms with their empolyers, and as a result in 1891 and 1892 they enjoyed privileges and a high rate of wages which were totally unprecedented in the trade. At the I present time, however, the trade in I:ancashire was more depressed than had ever previously been known.' The masters and the men had met and tried to come to an arrangement, but had failed. The masters had promised that the men would be paid the 1892 rates should the trade become better. It had been suggested to Mr. A. Greenwood that the men should appreciate the masters' position, and come willingly to their aid, and so stand by the trade which threatens to leave the country. In reply, Mr. Greenwood had admitted that the workmen were as anxious to retain the trade in the country as the masters themselves. But (referring to Lancashire) Mr. greenwood said there was as much ' difference perhaps between the position and the work- ing conditions of the Yorkshire men and the Lancashire men as there was between the Lancashire men and the Continental workmen. This was oneof the chief factors the leaders of the Lancashire men had rested upon to justify them in submitting to the reduction. They said that in submitting to the reduction—equal to 15 per cent.—they could earn as much and even more wages than the Yorkshire men.

Nine representatives of the Lancashire bottle makers held a conference with the Lancashire manufacturers at the Raven Hotel, St. Helens, on Tharsday afternoon. The National Conference, which was being held at the Royal Hotel, was practically suspendod, and at a late hour the result of the interview with the masters had not been reported to the delegates. It is supposed that the masters have made some offer, and that another ballot of the Lancashire men will be taken.

HUMAN P REY.

It is rare that you hear a soldier of the war boast of a shot which laid an enemy low. There were in- stances when he•could not help but realise that his bullet carried deatheaut the reflection is not a plea- sant one. He is in battle line to hold the ground-- to load and fire—to help wit a victory. If he killed, it was legalised murder. He had the spirit of a warrior instead of a hunter. There was no premedi- tation ; no cool, calm waiting and manceuvring to send a fellow-being into eternity. Beyond the earthwork the ditch. Beyond the ditch the murderous tangle called the abatis. Beyond the abatis those lairs called rifle pits. They were deep enough and broad enough for graves. Sometimes they were long enough for the corpse to lie at full length. At other times the body was found half doubled up, and the stiffened limbs caught at earth and stone and root as the body was lifted out. The rifle pit was the laio—the occupant a sharpshooter—a human tiger thirsting for blood, He was. not ordered to the work. Be volunteered for it. His rifle at home had brought down deer and turkey and bear—perhaps a panther. Be had brought down squirrels as they chattered at him from the highest limbs. He now creeps forward to a rifle pit under Cover of darkness to try his skill on human - prey. From this cover we can use the glass and bring the pit almost at our feet as the sum comes up. The hunter has been patiently. waiting. He has eaten his breakfast of pork and hardstack, washed it down with a sip from his canteen, and his rifle has, been cleaned and loaded. Rifles are used here—long, heavy rifles, with sights nicely adjusted. The pit has been dug so as to leave a sort of shelf for the hunter to sit on. In front of him dirt has been heaped up into a breastwork. The pits of the enemy are opposite, and only half shot away. Be- hind the hunters are earthworks mounting heavy guns. Three or four of the guns on either side cannot be worked because of thess hunters. Now watch our human tiger. The morning has been quiet thus far. He desires to locate the min opposite him—to test his promptness and skill, and to judge of the weapon he is armed with. He lays aside his own rifle and withdraws the ramrod. He then drops his hat over it, and carefully raises it into view above the heaped up dirt. It is lifted inch by inch, as if the owner was straightening himself up to get a view. • Crack ! Crack! The reports were from the rifle pits to hie right and left. rhe hunter directly opposite was not on the alert. The hat ie dropped for a moment. Firing has now begun all along the line. Not that the hunters can see each other, hut as a sort of challenge or defiance. Now the hat is lifted again. It is a trick played, every hour in the day. The hunter opposite waits until the brim is almost in view before he fires. Had there been ahead under the hat, the bullet would have crashed into it. Now begins the watching and waiting. Our hunter carefully digs at the earth before him until he has made a trench for his rifle barrel to rest in. Ills muzzle has been stained with mud, and 'neither barrel nor trimmings elistea in the bright sunshine. The hammer is raised, finger on trigger, and the hunter's eye glancing along sights. There is human prey over there behind the other dirt pile. If the hunter is flushed with pride because his bullet reached the heart of a panther in its spring, how will he feel when he knows that the leaden messen- ger has ploughed its way to the brain of a fellow being? , There is no excitement, no trembling, no hope rising up that the man opposite may lie close and escape death. Watch ! Wait! Watch ! If a hunter can watch and wait for an hour or two beside a deer trail, he should not complain of half a day here, where his expected I prey is human. An hour goes by. Our hunter removes his finger from the trigger am 4 his eye from the sights and leans back to rest for a moment. He mutters his disappointment. He even curses the obstinacy of his expected victim. Five minutes later, with hope renewed, he resumes his vigil. Ten minutes creepelowly by, and another half hour drags away. Ah, there is a speck or encouragement —a flitting, fleeting, shadowy something at the left of the dirt pile opposite. Oar hunter's eyes brighten, a grim smile covers his tae,e, and his heart beats a little , faster. The game is in sight asit were. One minute—two—three--four—five. There is not a quiver of an eyelash as he looks through the sights—the finger on the trigger seems made of iron. The shadowy something is seen again,and now it is identified. It is a faded,dirt-stained, and ragged old cap rising out of the rifle pit and above the little earthwork. The hunter opposite is wondering if the pit is occupied by living or dead. Be is making a movement fraught with peril but perhaps this is his•fiest man hunt. Up—up—up rises the cap. There is a human bead under it. Crack !' It is the report of our hunter's rifle, and he sinks down with a chuckle of satisfaction. He had caught sight of a sunbrowned forehead, bushy hair and a pair of eyes gleaming like those of a wild beast, and he knows that his bullet sped surely. As he sinks down the other rises to his feet, throws up his hands and falls in a heap, sounding his death cry as he falls. Killed! Shot through the head! One less enemy ! • And our hunter smiles and chuckles and feels that men must look upon him with awe and admiration! •

RAILWAY RATES-AT ST. HELENS.

Our St. Helens correspondent states that, from in- quiries made on Wednesday,that the borough stands in an enviable position as regards railway rates, and this is due largely to the efforts of the present Mayor, Mr. Arthur Sinclair. When the London and North-Western Railway Company purchased the old St. Helens Railway, by which prior to 1864 the St. Helens manufacturers had their chemicals, glass, copper, coal, and other commodities carried to the Mersey, the North-Western agreed to a 'special rate of charges, but when the Company, in conjunction with other companies,, sought powers to increase the rates, then they made an effort to bring St. Helens on an equality with other places. Mr. Sinclair took a foremost position in resisting this, and as a result the Commis- sion not only re-enacted the special rates, but inserted a clause that they sheuld be preserved to the town for ever. The benefit is shown by one instance—viz., whereas 33. 4d. per ton is charged between St. Helens and the Liverpool docks, only is. Bd. per ten is charged between St. Helens and tke Garston docks, and a still further decreased rate to Widnes. There are several small grievances still to be remedied, the principal one being the increase of glass to London and other places. The following is the clause in the Act of 1891 which is the safeguard for St. Helens traders :—" Nothing in this order shall affect the tolls, rates dues, and charges prescribed by the St. Helens Canal and Railway Transfer Act, 1964 (sections 12, 13, and 14), the London and North-Western Railway (New Works and Additional Powers) Act, 1867 (section 48), but the company may, in respect of the traffic there referred to, demand or take the tolls, rates, dues, or charges in excess thereof. Provided, forther, that nothing herein contained shall prejudice or affect any maximum rates or charges in respect of any new dock for the construction of which the company may seek powers from Parliament." An appendix fully sets forth the maximum charges allowed to be made.

ST. PAUL'S OIL U Rail, WARRINGTON.

ANNUAL TEA MEETING. The annual congregational tea meeting in eon- nectiorP with the above-named church took place on Monday evening in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- et., upwards of 330 persons being present. The tables were presided over by members of the con- gregation, and the tea, which was a very good one, was thoroughly enjoyed. After tea there was a musical entertainment,Mr.Robert Davies presiding, and being supported by the Vicar (the Rev. H. V.Pigot), Alderman Sutton'(Vicar's warden),Councillor Smethurst (people's warden), and Mr. T. Morris (sidesman). The Revs. T. M. R. Young-Hughes and J. Forbes (curates of ' the parish). the Rev. L. J. T. Darwall (Walton), and the Rev. J'. C. Pigot (St. Helens),were also present. The Cuarnatax, who rose amidst applause, then ad- dressed the meeting. In the course of his remarks he said he sometimes looked with dismay to the future.even In Warrington. They had large populations of working- 'nen and women growing up, and very inadequate pro- vision was made for the maintenance of religious fabrics, and it, therefore, behoved them to be as liberal as they could. (Applause.) The object of the present gathering was to assist the churchwardens of St. Paul's to keep the parish organisations going—the class rooms, mission rooms. &c., and by assembling together that evening they would have the satisfaction of knowing that they were doing something to carry on the good work in which the Vicar was engaged. (Applause.) He should like to see better provision made for the clergy' and for ministers of all denominations, for religious objects and for the maintenance of religious fabrics. Mr.Pigot had not been in Warrington long,but they must feel that he and Mrs. Pigot, Mr. Young-Hughes, and Mr. Forbes were deserving of their respect and admiration for the excellent manner in which they worked in what he might call an overloaded parish. (Hear, hear, and applause.) , The first part of the eoncert was carried out by mem- bers of the choir, who did their part admirably, and it comprised quartettes by Messrs. Scattergood, liarkinson• Sherlock, and Ratcliffe, a trio by the three first-named gentlemen, songs by Miss Oxley, Miss A. Oxley, Mr. Sherlock, Miss Armstrong, and Mr. Parkinson, a duet by Messrs. Parkinson and Sherlock, and selections on the handbells. There were several encores. Alderman SUTTON, in a humorous speech, regretted his inability to attend the church as reenlarly as he used to do, as he now lived out of town. However, his C 0 warden, Mr. Smethurst, wham he sincerely thanked for his services, relieved him of many of his duties, and was very earnest in his work in the church and parish. (Applause.) He proposed a vote of thanks to the ladieS for their services at the tea tables,and made complimen- tary references to the Vicar and Mrs. Pigot. Mr. Thomas MORRIS seconded, and the motion was heartily carried. who A vote of thanks was also passed to Mr. DaatieS, left at the conclusion of the programme, and on the motion of the Vicar, thanks were tendered to Miss 'West - well and Miss Smethurst for obtaining the trays aad subscriptions for the tea party and to the vocalists and instrumentalists for their services. The second part of the programme was entitled " MUSIC and Mechanism," and consisted of a series of tableau vivants, the figures being exhibited and explained by Mr. Robson Chorley. The following took part in these —The Misses Reynolds (Bank House School, Sankey-st.), the Rev. J. Forbes, Mr. James Henry Smethurst, Mr. Herbert Pigot, Master Richardson, Miss Pigot, MO. Adams, Miss Annie Smethurst, Miss D. Reid, Miss Itlicts Smith, the Rev. J. Forbes, Master J. Richardson, and Mr. A. H. Burgess. A vote of thanks to the Vicar and Mrs. Pioot, on the motion of Mr. Smethurst, seconded by Mr. W. Richard- son, concluded the proceedings.

RESULT OF THE PANAMA TRIALS.

HEAVY SENTENCES.

The Paris Appeal Court pronounced sentence on Thursday on the defendants in the Panama prosecution. Comte De Lesseps and his son Charles were each sen- tenced to five years imprisonment, MM. Fontane and Cottu to two years' imprisonment, and M. Eiffel to im- prisonment for two years and to pay a fine of 20,000 francs. The sentence on Comte de Lesseps was pro- nounced, of course, in default. M. Charles de Lesseps, Who is in custody,was much affected by the sentence. M. Eiffel, who seems to have received enormous sums from the Company, has given notice of appeal.

tLSTER " LOYALISTS " RUN SHORT OF

FUNDS.

The Ulster Loyalist Union has just made a special appeal for help, which appeal is signed by the Earl of Ranfurly and Viscount Templeton. It is as follows : " Owing to the late general election having exhausted our funds, ye are compelled to appeal at once for assis- tance, as Id fresh election might take place at any Moment. The Unionist interests we are fighting for are of vital importance to the Loyalists of Ireland, as well as to the welfare of Great Britain. The demand on us for speakers and other work has already commenced, and we shall not he able to cope with it in a satisfactory manner unless we have a liberal and immediate response to this appeal."

STRANGE CONDUCT OF AMERICAN , REPORTERS.

' Mr. Cleveland refuses to allow reporters to stand in the hall of his private house, tb watch his doings and those of his family, and to give information at the door about his visitors and their comings and goings. He insists on his right to privacy. • Probably no man in the 'United States, " The Nation says, has suffered so much A, swarm of reporters followed him into the country on °ls wedding trip, and encamped in tents or lay conceal- ed in the bushes, armed with field-glasses, around the - house in which he passed his honeymoon, "like African vages around the lair of a wild beast." He was pur- tued in the same way into the Adirondacks, when he "ad a cottage there in his vacation during his Presidency. " The tormentors lay in wait for him and his wife in the !surrounding womis, like the Iroquois or Pequots in the last century. They have dug up the hatchet against hills again, now that he has been re-elected and become once more a public man."

THE LIBERAL CANDIDATE FOR , GATESHEAD.

The Leeds Mercury " says :—Mr. Allen; who has been chosen by the Liberals of Gateshead to fight their battle, is the sort of man of whom by-passers in the street stop to exclaim "What a splendid-looking fellow." I.llr. Allen is, in fact, ohe of the most distinguished-look- IN men of our day. In height he reaches over six feet, ith shoulders like Hercules. He has no easy fight before him at Gateshead, and he will have as his oppon- ent Mr. Ralli, the Greek merchant, who unsuccessfully nnntested the seat at the general election, and who also Contested against Mr. John Morley at the recent by-elec- thoi at Newcastle, which is just across the river Horn Gateshead. But if Mr. Allen wins he will be the most lloticable man in the House of Commons. Mr. Allen is the, managing proprietor of the Scotia Engine Works, on !'ne river Wear, and recently became famous through Initiating a great labour experiment at his works. He called a meeting of his workmen, and asked them to Iftuont an sigh-hour day. The men were then working I j°ll.l BiX in the morning until five at night, with two teaks for meals. The proposal was adopted, and the Ten now work from half-past seven in the morning till t7e at night, with only one break for dinner. At the briset the men allowed Mr. Allen to reduce their wages 5 Per cent., but after six months' trial it was found hath More profit had been divided under the new system t their under the old. So Mr. Allen returned to the men eir 5 Per cent., and they now receive the old wages.

A WARRINGTON THEATRICAL DISPUTE. .

At the Manchester County Court on Tuesday before his Honour Judge Heywood, an action was brought by Mr. John Waller, theatrical agent, Bury New-road, Manchester, against Mr. F. W. Snaps, of the Theatre Royal, Warrington, to recover £4 155., balance of an account for services rendered and money paid. Mr. C. H. Wharton appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Browne, Warrington, for the defend- ant. It was stated that the plaintiff was engaged by the defendant's wife in December last to provide a company to fill up a vacant night at the theatre, and a guarantee was given that the company tbat might be under engagement that evening should be paid not less than a 7. It was at first proposed to play "Kathleen Mavourneen," but plaintiff found that the books for it could not be obtained. A bill was issued in which it was set forth that on Christmas Eve, December 24th, there would be an immense attraction '‘ for this night only " in the form of the production of the powerful domestic drama, " Two Christmas Eves ; or. " The Soldier's Trust," and the entertainment would conclude with the patriotic ctl in three tableaux, entitled "The *crelto of Midnight." The arliste lia.4 a rather MiXea "reception, owing, the plaintiff said, to the disorderly conduct of the audience, which consisted' partly of boys and partly of drunken med. So great was the noise that prevailed that " The Stroke of Mid- night," which was a pathetic piece, had to be with- drawn, notwithstanding that the members of the corn- pang were dressed for it, and a farce was substituted, entitled "The Secret," with the view of putting the audience in a better humour. The plaintiff paid seven performers 10s, each, also 15s. 9d. railway fares, 3s. to "supers," ss. for carriage of wardrobe, and £1 23. 6,1. for window lithographs, and charged Ll Is. for his own services, as be himself took a part. At the close of the evening he received £3, which was said to be all the money that was taken at the doors. He now brought the action to recover the balance.—ln answer to Mr. Browne, plaintiff denied that he promised to produce "Kathleen Mavourneen " and to take 55 per cent. of the profits. It was not the fact that there was only one actor in the company—a man named Buchananb—The evidence on behalf of the defendant was to the effect that the company were utterly inefficient and did not know their parts.—His Honour said that the shortness of the notice must be considered, and also the fact that there had been no time for rehearsal. 'lt seemed to have been left. to Mr. Waller to do the best he could in the time at his disposal, and, although the audience seemed to have been dissatisfied, there was not sufficient evid- ence that the plaintiff had failed to fulfil his part of the contract.—A verdict was accordingly given for the' plaintiff for the amount claimed and costs.

- DR. TANNER AS" POLITICIAN IN

E XTREMIS."

" BEDAD, I TOOK OFF MY COAT AND WAISTCOAT." The "Morning Leader" prints a delightful little in- terview with Dr. Tanner anent an incident which marked the opening of the new session : " Well, Dr. Tanner," I said, as I buttonholed the genial member; " will you tell me that to secure seats in the house you spread your coat and waistcoat on the seats in the house ? " "Yes, I did," said the doctor, with that charming frankness of his. " I could not see any other way out of the difficulty. You see Mr. Austen Chamberlain had arrived in the house at a very, very early hour, armed with quite as arsenal of hats. His intention, of course, was to secure seats for the family party," continued the doctor with a smile "and I suppose he had borrowed the family hats for the occasion. Well, I saw all this, and I was rather put out, for I had come down rather early to assert the claim of the Irish party to their time-honoured seats. My stock of hats was limited, and when I got to the end of my tether I was, as I thought, resourceless ; but, bedad, I took off my coat and waistcoat—as good as hats any day—and I spread them on the ,benches. Well, you may say it was unconventional and owtre ; but then, my dear sir, a politican in entremis won't stop at taking off the more superfluous of his garments. " But," continued the doctor, " a whole lot of lies has been told about that morning. It was said in the Press that I had a flask of whisky with me, and that i offered it as a form of consolation to the Tory members and Unionists. That allegation is nothing to me ; thank heaven my epidermis is thick enough to resist the attacks of the Press; but I want you to take note of this : It has been said in the Press that I offered that imaginary flask to Mr. Victor Cavendish, M.P. Now, Mr. Cavendish has come to me and protested against this. I don't know whether he protested against my supposed ministrations or against the contents of that non-existent flask, but I wish you would deny the statement in your paper. Nothing of the kind occurred. I had no flask, and therefore couldn't have offered a a drink from it to anybody."

WARRINGTON INFIRMARY WORKING- MEN'S COMMITTEE.

The annual meeting of the above-named Commit. tee WAS held in the Town Hall, on Saturday after- noon. The Mayor (Alderman E. Greenall) presided, and was supported by Mr. John Fairclough, J.P., and Major Pickmere.—The lists of contributions for the years 1891 and 1892,showed X4lO 178.7id.,f0r 1891 and £432 9s. 9d.,for 1892. These sums included proceeds of concerts in the Public Hall,and in Butterrnarket-street school, but the sum collected from the works in 1891 was £389 15s. 10., and in 1892 £421175. 5d., showing an increase of £32 2s. 3.&d.—The Chairman expressed his satisfaction at this increase, and pointed out that next year more money would be required to carry on the dispensary in consequence of the increase of accommoda- tion for the reception of patients, which would involve also the increase of the nursing staff.—Mr. John Roberts expressed his satisfaction at the increase el subscriptions, and of accommodation at the Infirmary, which he said would mean that where there had only been 22 to 24 beds there would now be 40. A great deal of the good work of the extension of the institution had been done by the vrerkingmen of Warrington who had subscribed since 1880 over £5,000, a creditable example for other towns.—Mr. Fanshaw proposed that those present be con- stituted the general committee with power to add to their number.—Mr. John Taylor seconded, and the motion was agreed to.—On the motion of Mr. Watkins, seconded by Mr. Hewitt, and supported by Major Pick mere, Mr. John Roberts was re-elected chairman, and Mr. Roberts thanked the meeting.—May and November were fixed as the months in Ns loch the contributions for 1893 should be called in.—Mr. Fanshaw proposed that the thanks of the Committee be given to the honorary medical and resident officers of the institution. He remarked that they could not think too highly of those gentlernen,who gave their services gratuitously to such a glorious cause, and did their duty so well.—Mr. Watkins seconded, and the proposition was carried.—Mr. John Fairclough moved a vote of thanks to Mr. E. M. Crosfield—whose absence was apologised for by Mr. Thomas Roberts who was acting for him for the occasion,—for his services as secretary during . he year.—Mr.Hibbert seconded this proposition, which was carried.—On the motion of Mr. Croskill, seconded by Mr. Walker, a similar vote was accorded to Mr. Joseph Taylor, assistant secretary.—On the motion of Mr. John Taylor, seconded by Mr. Wil- lett, the Chairman was thanked for presiding and for the use of the Council Room, and the Mayor expressed his thanks,assuring the meeting of the great interest he and his wife took in the Committee, and observing that ne took the more interest in matters pertaining to the Infirmary because he had two sisters-in-law both trained nurses. The meeting then concluded.

Zettero to the Obitor.

DOG MUZZLING IN CHESHIRE. Sir,--I see a recent order has been issued by which all dogs in the County of Chester must be muzzled, which is a name for inflicting a great amount of pain on the poor dogs. The Order 1.1.ay be a very wise precaution against rabies, but it appears to me so very strange that our poor dogs only suffer from that disease, when the game breeding se son is on. The gentlemen who put on the Order, are as a ~ule those who have game and wish to protect it in this way. Their dogs of course, are sport- ing dogs and, therefore, exempt from the Order. Trusting some stronger champion of the dogs than I, may take this matter up, I remain yours truly, FAIRPLAY.

THE DISMISSAL OF THE UNION BAKER. Sir,—lnotice in your issue of last Saturday a letter from Mr. Knowles with regard to the niggardly treatment re- ceived ty Mr. Cliffe from the Board of Guardians, after 25 years faithful service. I feel justified ill contradict- ing Mr. Knowles, as I was baker and cook at the War- rington Workhouse from August, 1871, until April, 1874. After this Mr. W. Mullholland held the situation two years and six months, and then Mr. Hildebrand was there over two years, so I cannot see how Mr. Cliffe could have served 25 years. I was the first paid baker that was employed at the Workhouse at 263. per week, from seven in the morning till eight at night, and I can- not see how Mr. Cliffe has been over-w.rked, as he had 30s. per week until a short time since.—Yours truly, JOHN HATTON. Baker. 7, Sparling-street, Latchford.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
BUDDIIISM AND CHRISTIANLTY.,

attention has been called to several letters upon the above subject, which appeared in your paper a few weeks ago. The writers must surely imagine that we are an exceedingly gullible public in Warrington if they could expect us in any seriousness to accept their settings of the "points of resemblance" between Buddhism and Christianity. I allude more especially just now to their parade of what they pretend to call facts. It is a pity that "Barry Esmond" and "Eclec- tic" had not first studied Max Muller's chapter "On False Analogies" in his lectures introductory to "The Science of Religion." It would have saved them from ' some well-merited ridicule. Not to dwell upon the glaring inaccuracy of "Harry Esmond" touching Chrishna whom he call. "a Buddhist God," but who was nothing of the kind (for Buddha eschews all such pretended deities). I should like to refer to several chronological "points" involved in your correspondents' letters. Eclectic" tells us that the Brahmans' claim for Chrishna that he lived 7,000 years ago. that is, over 5,000 years before Christ. But what evidence do the Brahmans give for any such statement? The ancient Egyptians said their dynasties went back thirty thousand years. but who believes it in this day? What then about the Brahmans—how far back do their reliable historical records take us Their most ancient writing is the Rig-veda, and the highest Sanscrit autho- rities tell us this cannot be fixed earlier than the 12th to the 15th century before Chi ist—and that its latest por- tions are as recent as the 2nd century B.C. Now what about Chrishna? His history is contained in what is called the second period of Sanscrit literature, namely, the Epic period, much more recent than that of the Rig- veda, and in that portico of it to which "Eclectic" re- fers—the Bhagavad-bita, an episode inserted in the great poem called the Maha-Charita. But what is the date of this episode known as the Bhagavad-gita? Not earlier says one of our greatest Sanscrit authorities, Sir Monier Williams, than the 2nd or 3rd century after Christ! In the face of this what becomes of " Eclectic's " insisua- tion that our gospel-story of Christ is nothing but a copy ,of the fable of Chrishna Now Ido not care to make any point of what some , initilent scholars may have assumed, that the stories about Chrishna in the Bhagavad-gita were suggested to the Brahmans from what they had heard of our gospels ' in the 2nd or 3rd centuries of our Era ; but I must refer to a series of most extraordinary impositions palmed off upon European scholars, and recorded by Max Muller in his chapter above referred to "On False Analogies." About 100 years ago Sir William Jones, the great orient- alist,and a certain Colonel Wilford were tricked by Brah- mans in India into the acceptance of certain passages as having a place in their sacred writings, and bearing a most startling similarity to certain characters of the old Testament, such as Adam and Eve, Noah, Abraham and others. The excitement created by these discoveries was inconceivable. Elaborate essays were written by such men as Sir W. Jones, and scholars all over Europe were occupied in the study of these remarkable extracts and theorising on their bearings upon sacred history. As time went on coincidences multiplied and were pointed out by the Brahmans ; " At last," Says Max "the coincidences became too great. The IfiStt. were again carefully examined ; and then it was found that a clever forgery had been committed, that leaves had been inserted in ancient MSS. and that on these leaves the Pundits, urged to disclose their ancient mysteries and traditions, had rendered in correct Sanscrit-verse all that they had heard about Adam and Abraham "etc. " But the mischief had been done," continues Max Muller, " essays had been written by Colonel Wilford and read all over Europe; they retained their place in the volumes of the Asiatic Researches, and to the present day some of his statements and theories " —founded, mark, upon these fraudulent MSS.—" con- tinue to be quoted authoritatively by writers oa ancient religion." (pp. 299-300). One would have thought that this instance of Brahma- nic fraud should have been a lesson for ever. But note the persistency of folly ; a work was published in Paris only a few years ago by M. Jacolhot under the sensa- tional title "The Bible in India, life of Jesus Christna." Max Muller seems to hesitate whether the work de- served his notice, then pours his scorn upon the produc- tion as he prefaces in these words, his obligation to do So :—" When one sees how its author shuts his eyes against all evidence that would tell against him, and brings together without any critical scruples, whatever seems to support his theory that Christianity is a mere copy of the ancient religion of India, mere silence would not be sufficient answer." Then follows his critique upon the work, which he exposes with unsparing hand, and concludes with a conviction that M. Jacolliot had been imposed upon by his Brahmin friends, and that "he ought to follow the example of Colonel Wilford,and publicly state what has happened" p. 329. Enough, I think, is hers given to emphasise the cau- tion with which such tales as "Barry Esmond" and "Eclectic" treat us to,shoulel be received. Christianity owes no more to the ancient religion of India than it does to the inhabitants of the moon. This has been de- monstrated a thousand times over on other most sure and certain lines of evidence and argument. As to the parallels which your correspondents fancy they see in the Brahmanic legends of Christina, and the historical events in the life of Jesus Christ, permit me to remark that I simply disbelieve that any genuine Hindoo writ- ing of very ancient date records the death and resurrec- tion of Chrishna in the manner described by one of your correspondents. Ido find a very different account from his, quoted by a high authority from the Vishnu-Purana " that he was accidentally shot in the sole of his foot by a hunter, who thought that he was aiming at a deer, and which brought his earthly career to a close." (See Chambers's Encyclopedia under "Vishnu.") Other as- sumed parallels are not true ; and as to those which are so, a very commonsense remark of Max Muller applies here when speaking of ethical similarities,that such in- stances found everywhere "in no way necessitate the admission that one religion borrowed from the other." Coincidences abound in all history, and in the com- mon events of daily life. The string of ordinary insignificant parallels cited by your correspondents (I refer here to facts or incidents) should merely excite one's derision or contempt. The fleeting features of most of the coincidences noted vanish the moment you come to trace and to compare them: They remind one only of those changing shapes-occasionally seen in the clouds ;—the features of some well known public char- actsr suddenly appearing and then dissolving into gro- tesque images as unlike to the first impression as that of a bull or a bear. Another word or two in closing. " Eclectic" would do well to make himself better acquainted with so large and important a subject, and to consult competent authorities before he undertakes to advise your readers to study.such an anachronism as the Bhagavad-gita with the object of showing how this production of the 2nd or 3rd century of our era is the source from Which our gospels of the Ist century are derived : and especi- ally let him feel assured that those of your readers who are interested In such questions as this are not likely to seek for enlightenment thereon from such moonstticken authorities as Madame Blavatsky.—Yours, JOHN DIGNUM. .7enlieth, Oth February, 1893.

AfH.LETICS.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

FOOTBALL.

It is o'er, and the glumness of my facial arsa would serve as a "dark cloud" of melancholy wherewith to decorate a "lobe" funeral. Warrington met Oldham on Saturday,and in doing so met defeat. It was disappoint- ing,for I, like many others, looked forward tD this match with pride—pride which swelled our bosoms so much that no clothing but an umbrella could be induced to fit, at times. Warrington were expected to defeat Oldham again, and by so doing hoist up another step in the com- petition list, and help to lift from the ground the fair flag of Warrington—the flag which has latterly been lowered by unworthy foes too often, and trampled in the mire by adverse critics. As one of my friends remarked after the match" Ah ! The team of my home—my birthplace, I thought to have seen you rise like an avalanche and "smash "'cm but your skill has only been of short measure and the obloquy of defeat has enshrouded you."

Bat for all that, I don't see anthing to go languishing and pining about. Oldham deserved their win (by three points) for they played a most admirable game their combinations, and individual finish being undoubtedly a grade higher than that of the home men. Anyone watching the game closely could easily see that it was almost entirely by the solidity of their scrimmages and packs, that Oldham did any scoring. But what they did was quite sufficient, although it could easily have been augmented had it not been for the careful though brisk defence of Boscow, assisted by Webster. At times, the strain on those two players was excep- tionally great. By sheer force the visitors several times brought the oval, yard by yard, until close upon the Warrington line, and time after time they were sent back to their own territory by a timely punt from Bos- cow.

This solid and united scrimmaging and packing on the part of Oldham did not mark that team as very clever in that direction ; it merely showed that Warrington have become rather too deficient. Discouragement may have been the cause of it, but upon my word, I felt like standing on one leg • and kicking myself with the other sometimes when I saw the indolent, and scattered way in which our home men west into scrimmage.

Then again there were several chances of scoring lost by Warrington players in their final essays—at the time when nothing but a little judgment was necessary. All these little shortcomings, which could have been overcome by a little care, decided the result of the game. To my thinking if Warrington had been in anying like their old form they would not have been beaten—even though it was only by three points.

Some good work however was done by Webster, Bate, Evans; and Fleesom. There are two expressive excla- mations which I heard on the field that I would like to repeat. They are "Oh, what it is to have a real con- scientious touch judge !" and "If Warrington had been in average form what would they have won by!" lam fain to answer like, the soughing wind without ? ? ? ?

We take this from the " Athletic News " : The affec- tion which some people have for football teams has often been demonstrated, but we think an instance which has come under our notice concerning some Oldham men beats the record. Things are not very prosperous in the hilly milly town, as most folk are aware, owing to the cotton dispute, and, consequently, money is very scarce but enthusiasm is still rampant, and we haVe been told that seven brave souls set out at Friday midnight to walk to Warrington to see the Oldham team play against the " Wire-pullers'" contingent: Their pilgrimage was rewarded by seeing a fine game and a brilliant victory. If there is another club with such loving adherents we should like to hear of it. Rem ! should we !

By the way, alluding to the Oldhamers being out of work reminds me of an explanation which I heard given for the splendid form of their team. It was said that almost every player had not been working for six weeks, and had been continually practicing during that time.

The' match to-day between Warrington and Tyldesley should be an interesting one. Both teams have played seven matches and scored eight points each, in the cup competition.

Swinton and Broughton Rangers met on Saturday to try their strength in the Cup Competition. As might have been anticipated, Swinton came out the winners— by 25 points to two. This leaves our weekly table of re- sults thuswise

It was a good game which our A and Oldham A played on Saturday. And it resulted in another win for our rising junier team, who are much more fortunate than the Ist team in keeping the scorer employed. They won by two goals, a try and four minors to nil.

I see that the Lancashire County Committee, for their match with Durham at West Hartlepool on Wednesday next, have again selected Boscow (back) and Jolley. Prophetic sporting oracles went so far as to predict that Boscow, after his last appearance with the county team, would not be chosen again. But the Committee have shown themselves to be persons of unbiassed judgment, and have courageously repeated their opinion that Boscow is the most capable back to be found in the county.

The remainder of the selected county team consists of :—Valentine (Swinton), Barratt (Salford), and ArCutcheon (Oldham), three-quarter backs; Bumby (Swinton) and Pallane (Manchester Rangers), half backs ; Kent, King (Salford), Melledew (Rochdale Hornets), Ashworth (Mossley), Atkinson kWigan), Roth- well (Swinton), Whittaker (Walkden), and R. P. Wilson (Old Boys), forwards. GYM.

I was glad to see that the Gymnasium Committee commenced the Saturday night displays on Saturday last. The bill of fare—a competition of three exercises, with the Manchester Simpson Memorial Club—was not at all overdue with what our versatile P.D. terms " Varigatedness." Of course we couldn't expect any- thing " taller "just yet, because the public of Warring- ton seem so backward at patronising this excellent institution, that it would be impossible to count upon a " house "large enough to defray any kind of expense. I think, however, if the Committee could carry out my old suggestion and procure the aid of a few Warrington 1 amateur entertainers, they could raise a much better display than Saturday night's at very little more cost.

One happy feature of the contest on Saturday, how- ever, was that in every exercise the general superiority of the Warrington men towered above their opponents like a telegraph posS, over a prop. This is not a patriotic boast. In this direction, however, I have a statement, which has gained currency, to deny. It is utterly un- true that the Warrington Committee spent a week in find- ing out the worst " eight " in Manchester so that they could bring them to Warrington and cheer the hearts of our people by giving the long delayed victory. It is a piece of contemptible slander.

HEATH SIDE SCHOOLS.

RE-UNION OF °Li) SCHOLARS AND FRIENDS.

This annual gathering took place on Wednesday evening, at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-st.. War- rington, when there were present about 150 ladies and gentlemen. Mr. Thomas Morris was the M.C. and he was supported by the head master of the schools (Mr. D. W. Finney), Messrs. F. G. Emson, J. H. Sherlock. F. Leigh, H. Leigh, E. J. Radford, and Joseph Irwin as stewards. In addition to the above there were present the Rev. Harry V. Piget, vicar of St.Paul.s, and Chairman of the Managers; Mrs.Pigot,Mr.Councillor Smethurst, Mrs. and the Misses Cox, Mr. J. H. and the Misses Smethurst, Mr. John and Mrs.Glover,Mr.F.Glover (Cronton), Mr. and Mrs. W. Richardson, Mr. and Mrs.J. Richardson, Mrs. and Miss Brundritt, Mr. S. Wright, J.P., Mr. and Mrs. Hannah, and Mrs. Amos (Grappelli. hall). Mr. Percy Randall's band was engaged, and gave the most unqualified satisfaction. Messrs. Chantry and Son were the purveyors, and it is only just to them to say that all the refreshments were excellent in quality and abundant in quantity. In the interval, after the first part of the programme, Mr. D. W. Finney came forward, and expressed the pleasure it gave him to see so many old scholars and friends present. He honed they would all thoroughly enjoy themselves. The Rev. H. V. Pigot said they must all feel glad to see Mr. Finney among them once more ; last year he was absent on account of indisposition. He then spoke of the im- provements and alterations which had been made in the Heath Side Schools of recent years, and invited them to pay a visit to the schools and see the changes which had been made. The aim and object of the managers was to give the scholars a good education along with religious instruction ; and he was sure they all looked back with pride and pleasure to their early connection with Heath Side Schools. The programme was then pro- ceeded with. Much credit is due to Mr. Frederick J. Emson for the trouble he took to provide for the comfort of the gathering, which was brought to a close in the early hours of the following morning. We must not forget a meed of praise also to Miss Pickton, Mr. J. H. Sherlock, and Mr. Emson for their songs at intervals during the evening.

THE SHIP CANAL WORKS NEAR WARRINGTON.

POINTS OF PROGRESS.

Since the disappearauce of the frost a few weeks ago, work of every character has been proceeded with on the ship canal about Warrington. But the work is at such a stage now that the labour of a few weeks is productive of little change in the general aspect of the surroundings, and consequently a person might walk from Acton Grange to Thelwall and see a picture bearing a great similarity to the one be might have seen three months ago. The great bridge at Acton Grange is completed; with the railway lines, plates, boxes, and telegraphic communications all fixed. The Company run light engines and trucks over the bridge and the adjacent length of line, to assist the metals and other things to set. This is likely to be continued some time, in accordance with the Act of Parlia- ment, and will then be followed by the running of goods trains by the L. N. W. Railway Co. These ballast trains are the only signs of active life about the work in that neighbourhood. The whole of the cutting from here down to Stockton Heath is finished with the exception of a few yards of untouched excavation beneath the bridge. And this will have to be left until the last thing. It is a kind of isthmus across the cutting connecting the engine sheds and works at Acton Grange, with the rail- way which runs down to Stockton Heath. At Wilderspool, where the Northwich-road is going to be diverted, the new swing bridge, over which the road is to be conveyed is nearing completion. It is fixed across the cutting, and the engineers and mechanics are quickly finishing their task. Within two months from the present the abutting property —the bay-windowed huuses on both sides of the road at the Warrington end of the bridge—will have probably disappeared. The houses have long since ceased to serve the purpose for which they were built—rural villas—and are now converted in- to 'lodging houses for the navvies and offices for the Company's engineers and surveyors. Until this new bridge is finished and the road deviation opened, there will be no other means of connecting the cut- ting on each side of the old bridge, except by the present subway under the road. Proceeding along, the next object of interest one comes to is the new cantilever bridge near the new Latchford station. This is the bridge over which Wash- lane is to be diverted to run on the south side of the Ackers pits instead of the north sides as at present. This has been necessitated by the canal running through that portion of the old Wash-lane, or Ackers-lane, just above the Baptist Chapel. The road has already been diverted just below the chapel and runs up a steep incline to the new bridge. Reaching the top of the road you see an arrange- ment which might be described as effecting that economical feat of " killing two birds with one stone." The new road will not simply continue over the bridge, past the house which used to be occupied by Mr. J.Dun, down to Lumb Brook. It has also been diverted at the bridge so as to pass the new Station yard, and lead on to Thelwall-road. Thus Latchford Station will practically stand at the end of a thoroughfare from lower Latchford, another from Lumb Brook, and another from Thelwall and higher Latchford. The two stone portions of the bridge are being connected with a centre girder. These girders are being erected on trestles at the bottom of the cutting, and when they are ready they will be raised to the height of the bridge and fitted properly. The Knutsford-road swing bridge has been almost completed. It it has been built parallel with the canal because, so near is it to the present railway line, that if it was built acrossit would probably touch it. The consequence is it will not be swung into its place until the new railway deviation is completed. There is nothing beyond this until past the locks at Latchford. Then crossing 'a stretch of the future bed of the canal, which has not been ex- cavated, one arrives at the commencement of a length of finished cutting extending to Rlxton. This is noteworthy, because it is filled right along with about six feet of water. This has been allowed to accumulate, the pumping engines having been removed, and it will be retain.* in the cutting until the length is opened. The erection of the hydraulic works at the Latchford Locks haa been completed; and the plant is being laid with all expedi- tion.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY.

THE REV. E. C. E. CARLETON ON DISESTAB-

LISHMENT.

At the weekly meeting of the Warrington De- bating Society, on Thursday evening, the Rev. E. C. E.Carleton read a short paper upon the Disestablish- ment of the Church in Wales.Mr.A.E.White occupied the chair and there was only a small attendance. The Rev. E. C. E. CARLETON said that the word dis- establishment as applied to the Church of England in Wales was an empty term, a term with no substantial meaning whatever ; and if it was not for the pressure of time he would gladly undertake to prove that it had no meaning at all, because disestablishment could not be effected in Wales unless it was also brought about in England; it was as impossible as it would be to effect disestablishment in any single county in England. They might weaken the church by the withdrawal of patronage but toe Welsh Church could not possibly be disestab- lished by itself because it was simply and purely a part of the English Church, and therefore so long as the Church in England existed as estab- -1 lished, so long would the Church in Wales exist as I established. The Church in Wales came under the same jurisdiction of the Archbishop of Canterbury, and while that dignitary existzd the Bishops of Wales would be isit Iled to attend his synod to consider the affairs of the Church. They would therefore see, that whatever measure was passed by the Government, they could not I pass a real Bill of Welsh Disestablishment. The word disestablishment would be a fallacy, although no doubt lit would be a convenient phrase under which some enemies of the Church could conceal their vindictive. ness. Mr. Gladstone might bring in a measure like the Irish Church Bill, which would simply amount to the withdrawal of patronage. But then the church was not bound up in any scheme of patronage, and although it could be impoverished by such a measure it could not be disestablished. Then again some people might say that the disestablishment would be effected by the dissolution of the House of Lords or by the discontinuance of the privilege which was enjoyed by archbishops in being allowed a seat amongst the peers. That could not effect the Church's establishment. Be con- cluded therefore, that Mr. Gladstone could not submit anything which would mean real disestablishment ;he might produce a measure under that name which would effect the church in material senses, but he could not touch its establishment unless he constructed a Bill of Intolerance,which,of course,he was not likely to do. The rev, gentleman's contentions were debated upon by Mr. J. Gandy, Mr. Horton, and Mr. Bruchemite. The latter contended that the disestablishment of the Church i would do away with many abuses which had tended to convert the church from a" place of prayer to a den of thieves." He alluded to the selling of livings as the chief of these abuses. He said that men totally **map* able of preaching an instructive sermon were able to. procure the livicg of a church, and keep men with true worth and talent out.

A FARM LABOURER'S FATAL FALL.

John Garrity, aged 25, a farm labourer to Mr. Hatton, farmer, Sankey, died in the infirmary on Wednesday. It seems that on the 30th of list month a chimney in deceased's bedroom on the farm took fire, early in the morning. The deceased got up hurriedly and dressed himself, slipping on a pair of clogs,but not fastening them. His brother put a ladder up the wall of the building,and the deceased ascended with a bucket of water. When near the top, however,he slipped,and fell to the ground. He sus- tained a fracture to the thigh, and was removed -to the Infirmary,where he died on the day named. An inquest was fixed by Mr. 9. Brighouse for 12 o'clock at the Infirmary, but the jury having assembled there, Inspector Rowbotham read a telegram from Mr. Brighouse stating that he was prevented from coming, and fixing 4 o'clock at the White Hart Inn, Sankey-at., as the time and place for the inquest. The inquest was held there at that time accordingly. Alfred and Martin Garrity, the deceased's brothers, and Mr. Haddon gave evidence. It appeared that the deceased. Alfred Garrity, and James Devine were in their bedroom on the farm, and sitting by the fire whensparks came down it, and as it was supposed to be on fire, the deceased thought sparks might fire the stacks. Witness put a lad- der up outside,and the deceased ascended to the roof and put a wet sack down the chimney. Witness carried al bucket of water up to him to pour down the chimney. The chimney was brick part of the way up, and then con- sisted of drain pipes put one on the top of the other. The deceased fell from the roof, and broke his leg, and the pipes were found broken. It was supposed that through these giving way when he had hold of them, and his clogs being loose, he slipped and fell. A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned, the jury expres- sing the opinion that all praise is due to the deceased for doing his duty in trying to save his employers property.

Mr. Dayitt boasts that his bill of expenses in con- nection with his return far North-East Cork is one of the lightest on record, his only disbursmnet being 10d., the cost of a telegram in which he acknowledged ,the notification of his return.

HOUSE COMMITTEE.

This Committee recommended that the chaplain be allowed the assistance of St. Mary's parochial choir, and its members be allowed to visit the Workhouse for the purpose of taking part in the Sunday services. The Committee also recommended that four applicants for the office of baker and cook be seen by the Board, and that they make a selection from them.—The minutes were adopted,on the motion of Mr. RATCLIFFE, seconded by Mr. DAVIES.—Mr. A JOLLEY here rose to speak but the Chairman inf-Tmed him that he was too late.—Mr. JOLLEY objected to this.—The minutes were adopted.

RELIEF OF ABLE-BODIED PERSONS.

The CHAIRMAN said there were five other exceptional cases in which the Relieving officer had allowed relief to able-bodied persons to the amount of 21s. The Chair- man proposed that the Local Government Board be appealed to to authorise this relief, as previously.—The Assistant CLERK said he had received a letter from the Local Government Board sanctioning the previous departure from the regulations in providing relief of this kind.—The Chairman's motion was seconded.—Mr. RATCLIFFE said he had never heard any seconder to the proposition.--The CHAIRMAN said this was rather unfor- tunate and said if Mr. Ratcliffe had listened to the Clerk—Mr. RATCLIFFE (interrupting) : It takes me all my time to listen to you.

THE VACANT NEWTON SEAT,

The Assistant CLER said an order had been received from the Local Government Board fora new election in the place of Mr. Hood-Wri4ht, who had resigned.

THE SURVEILLANCE OF THE WORKHOUSE.

The Assistant CLERIC read a circular received from the Local Government Board authorising the admission of Guardians to the Wprkhouse and schools at any time, also the appointment of ladies, not necessarily Guardians, as visitors. Mr. SHAW said there were one or more men and boys in the blind asylum that the Guardians paid for, and he thought it would only be right to have a committee to visit these institirtions.—The CHAIRMAN reminded Mr. Shaw that that was not the question.—Mr.W.lloconToN : We have practically had that in force since I have been on the Board, but I suppose now it becomes part of the consolidated orders.—The CHAIRMAN : It is in conse- quence of the action of this Board years ago that the Local Government Board are taking us as a pattern and making it applicable over all the country. Some of the old Guardians will no doubt take credit for instructing the Local Government Board. It WAS a considerable hardship that people could not get into the Workhouse except on fixed days.

Mr. W. Florrowron said at the last meeting the Board went into committee, and the reporters had the good taste, discretion, and decency to leave the room, but in one of the papers, in an account of the business of the Board, there was a paragraph setting forth what the business in committee was, which affected both Mr. Shaw as the person making complaints and an officer against whom they were made. This was a gross breach of privilege. There was no one but Guardians and officers in the room when the complaints were made. (A Voice :What paper?) The "Observer." Mr. Shaw made complaints against a certain relieving officer, and it was a gross breach of confidence to report the matter, and one which ought not to pass unnoticed. The Board had suffered from the same thing in the past, and it was time it was put a stop to. (Hear, hear.) There was no other public business. APIPOINTMENT OF A BAKER. _ _ . .

A COMPETITON AT THE WARRINGTON GYMNASIUM.

Several causes—the recent smallpox epidimic chiefly—have had a very detrimental effect upon the Warrington Gymnasium. The membership was very much affected, especially in the ladies' classes, but since the abet )ment of the epidemic the num- bers on the books bave grown to their usual strength. But there has been one feature missed throughout the season, and that is the series of fortnightly displays. On Saturday last, however, the first attempt was made to revive these pleasant entertainments, and it was rewarded with a fair amount of success. A competition had been ar- ranged between a picked " eight" of the home " Gym." and an" eight" from the Simpson Memorial Club, Manchester. The attendance of spectators, how- ever, wig not very large, and this was alluded to by the Chairman,Mr. McGregor,who,amongst other things, said that Mr. Kinsley, the director, was worthy of all the en- couragement they could give him in his attempts to benefit the institution. He had come amongst them as a stranger, and had had to make his way amengst them.—The judge for the occasion was Professor Renshaw, who is one of the best known judges in the gymnastic world. The first thing noticeable between the two "eights" was the superior physique of the Warrington men. Both in general agility and sheer muscular strength they were far ahead of their opponents. This was seen particu- larly in the first exercise—the dumb-bells. The maximum number of points in this exercise is 160. Out of that Warrington gained 150 against 118 for their opponents, who, by the way, were directed by their conductor in every exercise, whilst the home" eight" performed each without a word of "prompting." The second exercise was parallel bars. This is always a difficult exer- cise. With the exception of the horizontal bar it is the exercise which requires the most nerve and the most finely trained sinews. With the exception of one man the individual scoring of the visitors was below that of the home men. G. Tomes, Manchester, however, got NI points, the highest score for some really well finished evolutions. Amongst the homesters the highest scores were taken by J. Holmes, W. Walsh, and J. r Podmore, each of whom got 16 points; Bennion, Warren and Locker taking 15. A. Dickens, who made a very young substitute for G. N. Vernon, came out very ' promisingly, whilst J. Mottram gave an excellent dis- play for 12 points, and prnbably deserved more. The individual miring was thus :—Warrington F. C. • Bennion' 15; J. Holmes, 18 ; W. Walsh, 16 ; J. E. Warren, 15 ; J. Mottrarn, 12 ; J. Locker, 15 ;J. Podmore, 16 ;A. Dickens, 11 ; total, 116. Man- chester : J. Taylor, 9 ; W. Entwistle, 11 ' • C. H. Chappell, 14 ; T. Pearson, 9; G. Tomes, 18 ; 3.5. Hunt, ;J. H. Hacking, 13 ; W. Gasket!, 16 ; total 102. Warrington thus won by 14 points. In the next com- petition, rope climbing, there was not much difference between the two teams, although Warrington came in for 79 points against 54. This, of course, was merely a r trial of arm streng,th,and who more likely to have taken the highest score than Locker and J. Podmore? The individual scores were :—Manchester : J. Taylor, 5 ; , Entwistle, 4 ; Patterson, 7 ; Pearson, 6: Tomes, Th; Hunt, 8 ; Hacking, 61 ; and Halton, 10. Warrington : Bennion, 8 : Holmes, 9; Walsh, 9i ; Warren, : Mot- tram, 10 ; Locker, 12; Podmore, 10 ; and Dickens, 101. The entertainment concluded with an exhibition on the horizontal bars by the home conductor, Mr. J. Kinsley, and Mr. Wall, the visitors, conductor. Mr. Kinsley had an opportrinity of showing the public what he really can do as an all round athlete. Professor Renshaw said he considered the exercises had been very well done. They had not been show exercises, hut exercises that required considerable skill. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. Conper, a vote of thanks was accorded to the judge, and the proceedings termin- ated.

A Bradford correspondent is informed by Mr. Mad- docks, one of the new Syndicate proposing to take over the business of Sir Titus Salt, Bart.. Sons,. and Co., Limited, that the arrangements for the reconstruction of the firm are in such a position that the scheme is almost certain to be carried out. The whale estate at Saltaire is to be taken over by the Syndicate.

At Liverpool City Police Court on Wednesday, William Sockett, farmer, Crossens, near Southport, was fined £lO and costs for having diseased meat deposited for sale. Defendant had three times previously bt,en con- victcd for similar offences.

I'UOPOSED VEHICULAR BRIDGE ACROSS

THE MERSEY.

A quarterly meeting of the Cheshire County Coun- cil was held at the Chester Castle, on Thursday, under the presidency of Mr. Duncan Graham, and was well attended. One of the subjects under dis- cussion was the following resolution passed and forwarded by the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority :—" That this authority have had under con- sideration the question of a vehicular bridge across the Mersey between Runcorn and Widnes, and strongly urge the necessity for such a bridge, and express the hope that the County Council of Cheshire will adopt all necessary measures for ensuring its erection."—The Clerk said he had received a joint letter Lom the Town-clerk of Widnes, and the Law-clerk of the Runcorn Com- missioners asking that an early date should be fixed for a meeting of the joint representatives of the County Councils of Lancashire and Cheshire. —The Chairman explained that a conference had been held between representatives of both councils and of the local authorities of Runcorn and Widnes, and the con- ference was adjourned sine die t.. enable the local autho- rities tc. produce further evidence. Mr. P. Speakman (Runcorn) said that everything was in line now, and whenever it was convenient to the County Councils to hold the conference the local autho- rities were prepared to meet them.

Mr. Joseph Eeckett (Audlein) : What will the bridge cost ?

The Chairman : The estimate is £lBO,OOO. Mr., Beckett What proportion do they want us to pay.?

The Chairman : The proposal at present is that the two local authorities should pay £30,000 each, leaving the County Councils of Lancashire and Cheshire to pro- vide the remainder, whatever that would be. At the lowest estimate it would be £10),000—or£60,000 apiece. Mr. Eddowes (West Kirby) : One of the local authori- ties suggested that it should be a toll bridge, and that the local authority should take all the toll. (Laughter.) No motion was proposed, and the subject was dropped.

THE SHIP CANAL.

REPORT OF THE DIRECTORS.

The report of the directors of the Manchester Ship Canal Company' which was submitted to the meeting of shareholders in Manchester on Monday, states that the available borrowing powers on, the 3lst December were £253,480 and £369,600, the balance unissued of the first and second mortgage debentures respectively, making a total of £023,080. The report then goes on to say The above-mentioned balance of the first mvrtgage debentures has been taken up.since-the close of the half year. A bill has been deposited in the present session of the Parliament to enable the Company to Raise Additional Loan Capital not exceeding £2,000,000 for the completion of the undertaking at a rate of interest not exceeding q per cent, per annum. The Corporation of Manchester has also deposited a bill to authorise the Corporation to lend the Company any additional sums not exceeding £2,000,000, and it is also provided that any other muni- cipal corporations may contribute towards such further loan. The Corporations of Salford and Oldham have deposited bills to authorise them to lend the Company any sums not exceeding £1,000,000 and £250,000 respectively. .. . _ _

The Total Area of Land. purchased to date is about 4;513 acres, the purchase money for which, including compensation for interfer- ence, amounts to £1,133,459, in settlement of claims amounting to £2487,020. Applications continue to be made for the purchase ot surplus lands, and several plots have been sold by the Company for works at various points on the Canal. The Corporations of Man- chester and Salford are applying to Parliamer.t for power to purchase land belonging to the Company for the erec- tion of abattoirs and cattle lairages. The follewing is the report of the Executive Commit- tee :—" During the past half year. Satisfactory Progress . .

has been made with the general Zvorks, which are ex- pected to be completed by the end of the year ; that portion of the work which was let by tender has been pushed on with much viaour, and there is every reason to believe that the contractors will finish within the, time specified. The Five Deviation Railways are now finished. The deviation of the Cheshire Lines railway between Flixton and Liam has been passed by the Board of Trade, and goods trains are now being worked over the line. The Board of Trade has also in- spected the deviation railway between Glazebrook and Partington and the junctions of the other deviation rail- ways near Warrington. The works on the

Runcorn Section have been carried on in a satisfactory manner,, and al- though they could not be commenced as early as the. other portions of the canal, in 'consequence of the neces- sity of first making provision for the accommodation of the river Weaver traffic, it is anticipated that they will be completed concurrently with the remainder of the works during the present year. The tibods of last autumn brought down into the canal a very large quan- tity of cinders, ashes, and silt,and have thus augmented the quantity of material ,to be dealt with by dredg- ing. Two Powerful Dredgers, which were provided for in the estimate of June last, are now being constructed at the Salford docks, and will shortly be ready for work. If no further unforeseen contingency arises, there is no reason to believe that the coat of the works for the completion of the canal as set forth in the estimate of June, 1892, will be exceeded. During the half year 700,997 Tons of Traffic _ _

were carried over the opened portion of the Ship Canal, and although nearly thie whole of the present traffic has to be admitted free of toll the net- revenue has covered the working expenses. In addition to the above-men- tioned traffic 611896 tons of dredgings were carried. The quantity of traffic loaded, discharged, and transhipped at Saltport during the half year was upwards of 100,000 tons, and consisted of Runcorn (Bridgewater Canals) traffic, materials for the works, and Ship Canal traffic, such as timber, salt, sugar, pitch, resin, &c. TWo Regular-Lines of Steamers are now running between Saltport and London and Glas- gow respectively. There is a good field for private en- terprise in the erection of warehouses in Manchester and Salford, and it-is understood that several. schemes are being mooted. Those who are thinking of promoting such undertakings are reminded that the time when there will b 3 a large demand for storage in warehouses is near at hand. The Traffic- on the Bridgewater Canal . .

has been well maintained. The net revenue for the half year, after making the usual, deductions, amounts to £30,078 104. 24. The new traffic arrangements ren- dered necessary isy the temporary closing of the Runcorn dock entrances have been successfully carried out, and the traffic has been worked to the satisfaction of all concerned. In conjunction with a line of steamers the Blidgewater undertaking has entered into a through carrying business to and from London, rid Saltport ; and, though there were many initial difficulties, a large business is in course of development. The Board of Trade has held an inquiry pursuant to. the Traffic Act, 1888, as to the proposals of six canals and has promul- gated a draft provisional order which' if applied to the Bridgewater Canal would render much of the traffic un- remunerative. Efforts are therefore being made, jointly with the other canals interested to secure some modifi- cation of the order. In

Mr. George Hicks, one of the auditors, also retires by rota- tion, and is eligible and offers himself for re-election. SPECIAL MEKFING OF SHAREHOLDERS. ONLY' ONE MILE OF EXCAVATION.

A largely attended special meeting of the shareholders of the Manchester Ship Canal Company was held on Monday to consider the bill now before Parliament for raising additional loan capital and to extend the time for the completion of the canal. In the absence of Lord Egerton of Tattou, chairman of the Company, Mr. J. K. Aythell presided. The bill, he explained, provides for the raising of a further loan of two million pounds on mortgage of the undertaking at a rate of interest not exceeding 41 per cent. He submitted a motion approv- ing of the bill. This was seconded by Mr. S. R. Platt, and adopted. At the ordinary meeting, which was after- wards held, it was stated that in all probability the canal will be completed by the end of this year. Of the 351 miles of excavation only about a mile now Temains to be done, and this is chiefly at points where the canal is crossed by railways.

ADVENTURES OF A POSTCARD. -- -- EIGHT AND A HALF YEARS ON A JOURNEY.

A curious story of " delay in transmission" is sent to the" Daily graphic "by a correspondent. On the 25th of June, 1884, the writer posted a postcard addressed to Mr. Beetenson. of Westminister. Though the missive was marked" immediate." it appears to have been in the " W" district office from that date till the 31st of January, 1893. Then it was accidentally discovered and forwarded, for some reason not explained, to the Kentish Town office, whero it was received on the Ist of February. i Thence t was despatched on the same day, to Westininister, and on the third it returned to the sender, after n a,bsoce of just oight years and a half.

A CONSTABLE-ATTACKED, BY POACHERS.

A large party of poachers, armed with bludgeons, was encountered on Thursday night at Crewe Green,near Lord Crewe's park. They were surprised by Police- Constable Roscoe, but he was no match for the men, who Set upon him and roughly handled him. One of the sang threw a brick which struck the officer on the head, inflicting a bad gash. The men then made off.

THE COTI-ON CRISIS.

ENTHUSIASTIC STRIKE MEETING AT BLACKBURN. At Blackburn, on Thursday night, one of the most enthusiastic meetings of operative cotton spinners over held took place to decide whether they should support the continuance of the Oldham spinners' strike. About 800 members were present, and they resolved absolutely unanimously that the strike must continue The men are already paying a levy of 4s. a week to support the locked-out Oldham men, and the meeting resolved to increase this amount if necessary.

• GREAT BILLIARD MATCH.

£15,000 AT STAKE. On Thursday night the Victoria Club was crowded to witness a match between two prominent members for probably the largest amount ever played for, viz., £5,550, and before the game concluded, there was fully £15,000 at stake. The game comprised 1,000 up, be- tween Mr. T. G. Wilson and Mr. R. H. Fry, the well- known bookmoker, and formerly of Liverpool. The former conceded 300 points start, restricting Mr. Fry to the top left pocket and cannons, while Mr. Wilson took the other five receptacles and laid ten "monkeys" that he won. Full score :—Wilson, 1,000; Fry, 915,

ANOTHER DEEMING BEFORE THE 3LEi GISTRATES.

At the Birkenhead Police Court yesterday morning, Samuel Deeming, brother of the notorious Rainhill mur- derer, was brought up before Mr. Preston and charged with having stolen a number of tools, valued 10s. the property of Charles. Smith, bricklayer,, Liverpool. It appeared that Deeming had been employed by Smith in repairing the drains in a house at Lower Tranmere, in December last. When the work was completed Smith left some of the tools in the house, and when he returned for the purpose of taking them away, which he did a few days afterwards, he found several of the tools bad been stolen. He traced them to a pawnshop in Liver- pool, and ascertained that the prisoner pledged them there. Deeming now said he had taken the tools, having been driven to do so by want. He had no food, and he not got a bed where he could sleep. He was committed to the assizes for trial.

===== 0002601_18930211_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF MR. JENNINGS, M.P.

Mr. Louis J. Jennings, the member for Stockport, died on Thursday night, at his residence, 73, Elm Park Gardens, London. He had been ailing for two years, suffering from ulceration of the intestines. Mr. Jenn- ings was perhaps better known as a literary man than as a politician. He made a name as an independent and outspoken journalist in New York, and wrote "Eighty Years of Republican Government in the Un- ited States." He has for some time been reader to the great publishing-house of Murrays, and has looked after the interests of the "New York Herald "bureau in this country from time to time. He was 56 years of age, and he will be a distinct loss to the House of Commons, on both sides of which he was much respected. At the general election the parties in Stockport stood as follows on the poll :—Joseph Leigh (L), 5,202 ; L. J. Jennings (C.), 4,986 ; Major Sharp Hulme (L), 4,87?; Hon. P. Bowes Lyon (C), 4,681. Although no decision had been come to, it has been assumed by most people in the town that Major Hume will be adopted as EL Liberal candidte for Stockport on the ncx,. Quolttwity.

Isling- ton, in Liverpool, on TUESDAY, the twenty-eighth day of February instant, at eleven o'clock in the forenoon preci. e y.
